### The Collection of Antiquities / 古物陈列室
===Original===

There stands a house at a corner of a street, in the middle of a town, in one of the least important prefectures in France, but the name of the street and the name of the town must be suppressed here. Everyone will appreciate the motives of this sage reticence demanded by convention; for if a writer takes upon himself the office of annalist of his own time, he is bound to touch on many sore subjects. The house was called the Hôtel d’Esgrignon; but let d’Esgrignon be considered a mere fancy name, neither more nor less connected with real people than the conventional Belval, Floricour, or Derville of the stage, or the Adalberts and Mombreuses of romance. After all, the names of the principal characters will be quite as much disguised; for though in this history the chronicler would prefer to conceal the facts under a mass of contradictions, anachronisms, improbabilities, and absurdities, the truth will out in spite of him. You uproot a vine-stock, as you imagine, and the stem will send up lusty shoots after you have ploughed your vineyard over.
The “Hôtel d’Esgrignon” was nothing more nor less than the house in which the old Marquis lived; or, in the style of ancient documents, Charles Marie Victor Ange Carol, Marquis d’Esgrignon. It was only an ordinary house, but the townspeople and tradesmen had begun by calling it the Hôtel d’Esgrignon in jest, and ended after a score of years by giving it that name in earnest.
The name of Carol, or Karawl, as the Thierrys would have spelt it, was glorious among the names of the most powerful chieftains of the Northmen who conquered Gaul and established the feudal system there. Never had Carol bent his head before King or Communes, the Church or Finance. Entrusted in the days of yore with the keeping of a French March, the title of marquis in their family meant no shadow of imaginary office; it had been a post of honor with duties to discharge. Their fief had always been their domain. Provincial nobles were they in every sense of the word; they might boast of an unbroken line of great descent; they had been neglected by the court for two hundred years; they were lords paramount in the estates of a province where the people looked up to them with superstitious awe, as to the image of the Holy Virgin that cures the toothache.

===Chinese===

在法国一个最无足轻重的省份里，有座小城，城中一条街的拐角上矗立着一所宅子；至于街名和城名，在此必须略去。谁都会体谅这番依惯例所需的明智缄默的苦衷；因为一个作家既然担当起为本时代作史的职分，就难免要触及许多痛处。这座宅子名叫埃斯格里尼翁府；不过，但愿“埃斯格里尼翁”只被当作一个虚拟的姓氏，它与真实人物的关系，不多不少，恰似舞台上惯用的贝尔瓦尔、弗洛里古尔或德维尔，又像小说里的阿达尔贝和蒙布勒斯。归根结底，书中主要人物的名字也同样经过遮掩；因为在这部历史里，执笔者尽管宁愿用一大堆矛盾、时代错置、不可能与荒诞把事实掩盖起来，真相仍会不听他的话显露出来。你自以为把一株葡萄树连根拔了，等你把葡萄园翻耕一遍之后，残茎却又抽出茁壮的新芽来。
所谓“埃斯格里尼翁府”，无非就是老侯爵居住的那所房子；用古老文书的体例来称呼，便是夏尔·玛丽·维克托·安热·卡罗尔，德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵。这原是一所普通的房子，可是城里的居民和商贾起初打趣地称它为埃斯格里尼翁府，二十年之后，便一本正经地这样称呼它了。
卡罗尔这个姓氏——照蒂埃里学派的拼法该写作“卡拉瓦尔”——在征服高卢、在高卢建立封建制度的那些最强悍的北方首领的姓氏之中，也曾显赫一时。卡罗尔家从不曾在国王或公社面前、在教会或财阀面前低过头。往昔，这个家族受命镇守法兰西的一处边疆；他们家的“侯爵”称号绝非虚设的空名，而是一个负有职守的荣耀职位。他们的采邑历来就是自家的产业。他们是完全意义上的外省贵族；可以夸耀世系绵延、门第高贵而从未断绝；宫廷冷落了他们两百年；他们是一省领地上的至尊领主，百姓带着迷信的敬畏仰望他们，如同仰望那尊能治牙痛的圣母像。

===Original===

The house of d’Esgrignon, buried in its remote border country, was preserved as the charred piles of one of Caesar’s bridges are maintained intact in a river bed. For thirteen hundred years the daughters of the house had been married without a dowry or taken the veil; the younger sons of every generation had been content with their share of their mother’s dower and gone forth to be captains or bishops; some had made a marriage at court; one cadet of the house became an admiral, a duke, and a peer of France, and died without issue. Never would the Marquis d’Esgrignon of the elder branch accept the title of duke.
“I hold my marquisate as His Majesty holds the realm of France, and on the same conditions,” he told the Constable de Luynes, a very paltry fellow in his eyes at that time.
You may be sure that d’Esgrignons lost their heads on the scaffold during the troubles. The old blood showed itself proud and high even in 1789. The Marquis of that day would not emigrate; he was answerable for his March. The reverence in which he was held by the countryside saved his head; but the hatred of the genuine sansculottes was strong enough to compel him to pretend to fly, and for a while he lived in hiding. Then, in the name of the Sovereign People, the d’Esgrignon lands were dishonored by the District, and the woods sold by the Nation in spite of the personal protest made by the Marquis, then turned forty. Mlle. d’Esgrignon, his half-sister, saved some portions of the fief, thanks to the young steward of the family, who claimed on her behalf the *partage de présuccession*, which is to say, the right of a relative to a portion of the émigré’s lands. To Mlle. d’Esgrignon, therefore, the Republic made over the castle itself and a few farms. Chesnel, the faithful steward, was obliged to buy in his own name the church, the parsonage house, the castle gardens, and other places to which his patron was attached﻿—the Marquis advancing the money.
The slow, swift years of the Terror went by, and the Marquis, whose character had won the respect of the whole country, decided that he and his sister ought to return to the castle and improve the property which Maître Chesnel﻿—for he was now a notary﻿—had contrived to save for them out of the wreck. Alas!

===Chinese===

德·埃斯格里尼翁家族深藏在偏远的边地，就像恺撒某座桥被烧焦的桥桩完好地保存在河床上一样保存了下来。一千三百年来，这个家族的女儿们出嫁不带嫁妆，便去出家修道；每一代的次子们满足于母亲妆奁里自己的一份，出门去当军官或做主教；有的人在宫廷里结了亲；族中一个次子后来当了海军上将、公爵和法兰西贵族院议员，身后没有子嗣。而长房的德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵，从不肯接受公爵的称号。
“我的侯爵领地，与陛下持有法兰西王国的领地一样，条件也相同。”他对陆军统帅德·吕伊纳说——在他眼里，此人当年不过是个十分猥琐的角色。
尽可放心，在动乱年代里，德·埃斯格里尼翁家的人并没有把头颅送上断头台。这古老的血统直到1789年还显得高傲倔强。当年的侯爵不肯流亡；他负责守卫他的边疆。乡野对他的敬重救了他的脑袋；可是真正无套裤汉的仇恨也够强烈，逼得他只得佯装出逃，有一阵子躲藏度日。随后，区里以主权人民的名义玷污了德·埃斯格里尼翁家的土地；国家不顾年过四十的侯爵亲口抗议，把树林卖掉了。他的异母妹妹德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐靠家族年轻的管家帮忙——他代她主张“预分继承权”，即亲属对流亡者土地享有部分权利——保住了采邑的几处产业。于是，共和国把城堡本身和几处田庄判给了德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐。忠心的管家谢内尔只好用自己的名义买下教堂、神甫住宅、城堡花园，以及主人恋恋不舍的另外几处地方——钱由侯爵垫付。
恐怖岁月那些迟缓而又飞快的年头过去了。侯爵的为人赢得了全乡的敬重，他决定带着妹妹回到城堡，去经营谢内尔师傅——他如今已当上公证人——从废墟中为他们抢救出来的产业。唉！

===Original===

was not the plundered and dismantled castle all too vast for a lord of the manor shorn of all his ancient rights; too large for the landowner whose woods had been sold piecemeal, until he could scarce draw nine thousand francs of income from the pickings of his old estates?
It was in the month of October 1800 that Chesnel brought the Marquis back to the old feudal castle, and saw with deep emotion, almost beyond his control, his patron standing in the midst of the empty courtyard, gazing round upon the moat, now filled up with rubbish, and the castle towers razed to the level of the roof. The descendant of the Franks looked for the missing Gothic turrets and the picturesque weather vanes which used to rise above them; and his eyes turned to the sky, as if asking of heaven the reason of this social upheaval. No one but Chesnel could understand the profound anguish of the great d’Esgrignon, now known as Citizen Carol. For a long while the Marquis stood in silence, drinking in the influences of the place, the ancient home of his forefathers, with the air that he breathed; then he flung out a most melancholy exclamation.
“Chesnel,” he said, “we will come back again some day when the troubles are over; I could not bring myself to live here until the edict of pacification has been published; *they* will not allow me to set my scutcheon on the wall.”
He waved his hand toward the castle, mounted his horse, and rode back beside his sister, who had driven over in the notary’s shabby basket-chaise.
The Hôtel d’Esgrignon in the town had been demolished; a couple of factories now stood on the site of the aristocrat’s house. So Maître Chesnel spent the Marquis’ last bag of louis on the purchase of the old-fashioned building in the square, with its gables, weathervane, turret, and dovecote. Once it had been the courthouse of the bailiwick, and subsequently the présidial; it had belonged to the d’Esgrignons from generation to generation; and now, in consideration of five hundred louis d’or, the present owner made it over with the title given by the Nation to its rightful lord. And so, half in jest, half in earnest, the old house was christened the Hôtel d’Esgrignon.
In 1800 little or no difficulty was made over erasing names from the fatal list, and some few émigrés began to return.

===Chinese===

这座被劫掠一空、拆毁殆尽的城堡，对于一个被剥夺了一切古老权利的领主来说，岂不是太宽敞了吗？对一个树林被零敲碎打地卖尽、从旧领地的残余里勉强只收得到9000法郎年息的地主来说，岂不是太大了吗？
1800年10月，谢内尔把侯爵带回了那座古老的封建城堡。他看着主人站在空荡荡的院子中央，环顾已被垃圾填平的护城河和削得与屋顶齐平的城堡塔楼，情绪激动得几乎不能自持。这位法兰克人的后裔寻找失去的哥特式小尖塔和原先高踞其上的别致风信鸡；他举目望天，仿佛在向上天质问这场社会巨变的缘由。除了谢内尔，谁也体会不到这位如今被称作“卡罗尔公民”的德·埃斯格里尼翁大人物的深创巨痛。侯爵久久默然伫立，把此地——祖先的古宅——的气息连同呼吸的空气一起纳入胸中；然后，他迸出一声至为凄怆的感叹。
“谢内尔，”他说，“等动乱结束，我们总有一天还要回来；和平敕令颁布之前，我下不了决心住到这里来；他们不许我把纹章安上墙头。”
他朝城堡挥了挥手，跨上马，在妹妹身旁策马回城；她是坐公证人的那辆破篷车来的。
城里的埃斯格里尼翁府已被拆毁；贵族宅邸的旧址上如今立着两家工厂。于是谢内尔师傅花掉侯爵最后一袋金路易，买下广场上那所老式房屋，连同山墙、风信鸡、小塔和鸽舍。它从前是邑法院的院署，后来又做过大理院附属法院；世世代代都属于德·埃斯格里尼翁家；如今，现业主收下500金路易，把它连同国家授予合法领主的文书一并出让。就这样，半是玩笑，半是当真，这座老宅得名埃斯格里尼翁府。
1800年，把名字从那道催命名单上勾销已无甚困难，少数流亡者开始返回法国。

===Original===

Among the very first nobles to come back to the old town were the Baron de Nouastre and his daughter. They were completely ruined. M. d’Esgrignon generously offered them the shelter of his roof; and in his house, two months later, the Baron died, worn out with grief. The Nouastres came of the best blood in the province; Mlle. de Nouastre was a girl of two-and-twenty; the Marquis d’Esgrignon married her to continue his line. But she died in childbirth, a victim to the unskilfulness of her physician, leaving, most fortunately, a son to bear the name of the d’Esgrignons. The old Marquis﻿—he was but fifty-three, but adversity and sharp distress had added months to every year﻿—the poor old Marquis saw the death of the loveliest of human creatures, a noble woman in whom the charm of the feminine figures of the sixteenth century lived again, a charm now lost save to men’s imaginations. With her death the joy died out of his old age. It was one of those terrible shocks which reverberate through every moment of the years that follow. For a few moments he stood beside the bed where his wife lay, with her hands folded like a saint, then he kissed her on the forehead, turned away, drew out his watch, broke the mainspring, and hung it up beside the hearth. It was eleven o’clock in the morning.
“Mlle. d’Esgrignon,” he said, “let us pray God that this hour may not prove fatal yet again to our house. My uncle the archbishop was murdered at this hour; at this hour also my father died﻿—”
He knelt down beside the bed and buried his face in the coverlet; his sister did the same, in another moment they both rose to their feet. Mlle. d’Esgrignon burst into tears; but the old Marquis looked with dry eyes at the child, round the room, and again on his dead wife. To the stubbornness of the Frank he united the fortitude of a Christian.
These things came to pass in the second year of the nineteenth century. Mlle. d’Esgrignon was then twenty-seven years of age. She was a beautiful woman. An ex-contractor for forage to the armies of the Republic, a man of the district, with an income of six thousand francs, persuaded Chesnel to carry a proposal of marriage to the lady. The Marquis and his sister were alike indignant with such presumption in their man of business, and Chesnel was almost heartbroken; he could not forgive himself for yielding to the Sieur du Croisier’s blandishments.

===Chinese===

最早回到这座老城的贵族当中，有德·努瓦斯特男爵和他的女儿。他们已经彻底破产。德·埃斯格里尼翁先生慷慨地让他们住在自己家里；两个月后，男爵忧愤交加，死在他的屋檐下。努瓦斯特家出身于本省最高贵的血统；德·努瓦斯特小姐年方22岁；德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵为了延续后嗣娶了她。不料她死于难产，成了医生医术不精的牺牲品，所幸留下一个男孩，承袭德·埃斯格里尼翁家的姓氏。老侯爵——他才53岁，可是厄运和深重的苦难给每一年都增添了好些岁月——可怜的老侯爵眼看着这最可爱的人死去：一位贵妇，十六世纪女性风姿在她身上还了魂，那种风韵如今只存在于人们的想象之中。随着她的死，他晚年的欢乐也熄灭了。这是那种可怕的打击之一，余波震荡在此后岁月的每一个时刻。他在亡妻床边伫立片刻——她双手交叠，宛如圣女——然后吻了吻她的额头，转过身去，掏出怀表，折断发条，把它挂在壁炉旁边。那是早晨11点。
“德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐，”他说，“让我们祈求上帝，但愿这个时辰不再是我们家的凶时。我的叔祖大主教就是在这个时辰遇害的；也是在这个时辰，我的父亲咽了气——”
他跪在床边，把脸埋进床被里；妹妹也照样跪下；片刻之后，两人一同起身。德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐放声大哭；老侯爵却眼眶干涩，看看孩子，环顾房间，又望一望亡妻。法兰克人的倔强与基督徒的坚忍在他身上兼而有之。
这些事发生在十九世纪的第二年。德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐时年27岁，是个美丽的女人。本地区有个人——共和国军队军需草料承包商出身，有6000法郎年金——说动谢内尔替他向小姐求婚。侯爵和妹妹对他们的事务经办人竟有这般觊觎同样感到愤慨；谢内尔则几乎心碎；他不能原谅自己竟听信了杜·克鲁瓦西耶先生的甜言蜜语。

===Original===

The Marquis’ manner with his old servant changed somewhat; never again was there quite the old affectionate kindliness, which might almost have been taken for friendship. From that time forth the Marquis was grateful, and his magnanimous and sincere gratitude continually wounded the poor notary’s feelings. To some sublime natures gratitude seems an excessive payment; they would rather have that sweet equality of feeling which springs from similar ways of thought, and the blending of two spirits by their own choice and will. And Maître Chesnel had known the delights of such high friendship; the Marquis had raised him to his own level. The old noble looked on the good notary as something more than a servant, something less than a child; he was the voluntary liege man of the house, a serf bound to his lord by all the ties of affection. There was no balancing of obligations; the sincere affection on either side put them out of the question.
In the eyes of the Marquis, Chesnel’s official dignity was as nothing; his old servitor was merely disguised as a notary. As for Chesnel, the Marquis was now, as always, a being of a divine race; he believed in nobility; he did not blush to remember that his father had thrown open the doors of the salon to announce that “My Lord Marquis is served.” His devotion to the fallen house was due not so much to his creed as to egoism; he looked on himself as one of the family. So his vexation was intense. Once he had ventured to allude to his mistake in spite of the Marquis’ prohibition, and the old noble answered gravely﻿—“Chesnel, before the troubles you would not have permitted yourself to entertain such injurious suppositions. What can these new doctrines be if they have spoiled *you*?”
Maître Chesnel had gained the confidence of the whole town; people looked up to him; his high integrity and considerable fortune contributed to make him a person of importance. From that time forth he felt a very decided aversion for the Sieur du Crosier; and though there was little rancor in his composition, he set others against the sometime forage-contractor. Du Croisier, on the other hand, was a man to bear a grudge and nurse a vengeance for a score of years. He hated Chesnel and the d’Esgrignon family with the smothered, all-absorbing hate only to be found in a country town.

===Chinese===

侯爵对待老仆人的态度有了几分改变；从前那种近乎友谊的亲切温情再也不能完好如初了。从那时起，侯爵心怀感激，而他那宽宏而真诚的感激之情，却不断刺伤可怜公证人的心。对某些高尚的天性而言，感激仿佛是一种过分的酬报；他们宁愿要那种由思想方式相近而生的甜美的平等情谊，要两颗心灵出于自愿的结合。谢内尔师傅领略过这种崇高友谊的欢愉；侯爵曾把他抬举到与自己并肩的地位。老贵族把这位好公证人看得比仆人高些，比儿子低些；他是这户人家自愿效忠的封臣，一个被种种情感纽带系于领主的农奴。彼此之间谈不上什么恩义账目；双方真挚的情谊把这一切抛在了一边。
在侯爵眼里，谢内尔的官职体面算不了什么；他的老仆人不过是扮成了公证人罢了。而在谢内尔心中，侯爵如今一如既往，是神裔般的生命；他信仰贵族政治；想起自己的父亲曾敞开客厅的门通报“侯爵爵爷，饭已备好”，他并不脸红。他对这个败落门第的忠诚，与其说出于信念，不如说出于私心；他把自己视为这个家族的一分子。所以他的懊恼才如此深切。有一次，他不顾侯爵的禁令，斗胆暗示自己办下的错事，老贵族严肃地答道：“谢内尔，在动乱之前，你是绝不会容许自己生出这种有辱于人的猜疑的。这些新学说究竟是什么东西，竟连你也带坏了？”
谢内尔师傅赢得了全城的信任；人们敬重他；他的清白人品与可观家产使他成为一位要人。从那时起，他对杜·克鲁瓦西耶先生怀着十分决绝的厌恶；虽然他的禀性中很少有怨恨，他还是让别人也对这位昔日的草料承包商疏远起来。而杜·克鲁瓦西耶却是那种能记仇二十年、把报复慢慢滋养的人。他恨谢内尔，恨德·埃斯格里尼翁全家，怀着那种压抑在心、吞噬一切的仇恨——这种仇恨只有在外省小城里才能见到。

===Original===

His rebuff had simply ruined him with the malicious provincials among whom he had come to live, thinking to rule over them. It was so real a disaster that he was not long in feeling the consequences of it. He betook himself in desperation to a wealthy old maid, and met with a second refusal. Thus failed the ambitious schemes with which he had started. He had lost his hope of a marriage with Mlle. d’Esgrignon, which would have opened the Faubourg Saint-Germain of the province to him; and after the second rejection, his credit fell away to such an extent that it was almost as much as he could do to keep his position in the second rank.
In 1805, M. de la Roche-Guyon, the oldest son of an ancient family which had previously intermarried with the d’Esgrignons, made proposals in form through Maître Chesnel for Mlle. Marie Armande Clair d’Esgrignon. She declined to hear the notary.
“You must have guessed before now that I am a mother, dear Chesnel,” she said; she had just put her nephew, a fine little boy of five, to bed.
The old Marquis rose and went up to his sister, but just returned from the cradle; he kissed her hand reverently, and as he sat down again, found words to say:
“My sister, you are a d’Esgrignon.”
A quiver ran through the noble girl; the tears stood in her eyes. M. d’Esgrignon, the father of the present Marquis, had married a second wife, the daughter of a farmer of taxes ennobled by Louis XIV. It was a shocking mésalliance in the eyes of his family, but fortunately of no importance, since a daughter was the one child of the marriage. Armande knew this. Kind as her brother had always been, he looked on her as a stranger in blood. And this speech of his had just recognized her as one of the family.
And was not her answer the worthy crown of eleven years of her noble life? Her every action since she came of age had borne the stamp of the purest devotion; love for her brother was a sort of religion with her.
“I shall die Mlle. d’Esgrignon,” she said simply, turning to the notary.
“For you there could be no fairer title,” returned Chesnel, meaning to convey a compliment. Poor Mlle. d’Esgrignon reddened.
“You have blundered, Chesnel,” said the Marquis, flattered by the steward’s words, but vexed that his sister had been hurt. “A d’Esgrignon may marry a Montmorency; their descent is not so pure as ours.

===Chinese===

这番碰壁使他在那些刻薄的外省人中间彻底毁了名声——他原是到这些人中间来安家，指望统治他们的。这场灾祸如此真切，他不久便尝到了后果。他绝望之余转而追求一位有钱的老姑娘，又遭到第二次拒绝。他起初的野心盘算就此全盘落空。他失去了与德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐结婚的希望——那桩婚事本可为他打开本省的圣日耳曼区；第二次遭拒之后，他的信用一落千丈，几乎连保住第二流的地位都很勉强。
1805年，德·拉罗什-居荣先生——一个古老家族的长子，该家族先前曾与德·埃斯格里尼翁家联姻——托谢内尔师傅正式向玛丽·阿尔芒德·克莱尔·德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐求婚。她不肯听公证人说下去。
“你早该猜到了，我就是一位母亲，亲爱的谢内尔。”她说；她刚把侄儿——一个五岁的漂亮小男孩——安顿睡下。
老侯爵站起身，走到刚离开摇篮的妹妹跟前；他恭敬地吻了吻她的手，重新坐下时，说出一句话来：“我的妹妹，你是埃斯格里尼翁家的人。”
高贵的姑娘浑身一颤；泪珠涌上她的眼睛。当今侯爵的父亲德·埃斯格里尼翁先生续娶的第二位妻子，是路易十四册封贵族的一位包税人的女儿。在这家人看来，这是一桩骇人听闻的门不当户不对的婚姻，所幸无关紧要，因为这段婚姻只生了一个女儿。阿尔芒德知道这层底细。哥哥一向待她仁厚，却始终把她看作血缘上的外人。而他刚才这句话，承认了她也是这家族的一员。
她的回答，岂不正是她11年高贵生涯当之无愧的冠冕？自成年以来，她的一举一动都印着最纯粹的奉献的印记；对兄长的爱，在她是一种宗教。
“我要以德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐的身份终此一生。”她转向公证人，平静地说。
“对您来说，再没有比这更美的名衔了。”谢内尔回答，本想献一句殷勤。可怜的德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐红了脸。
“你说错话了，谢内尔，”侯爵说，管家的话让他受用，妹妹受了委屈又令他不快。“埃斯格里尼翁家的人尽可以娶一位蒙特莫朗西；他们的家系不如我们的纯正。

===Original===

The d’Esgrignons bear or, two bends, gules,” he continued, “and nothing during nine hundred years has changed their scutcheon; as it was at first, so it is today. Hence our device, *Cil est nostre*, taken at a tournament in the reign of Philip Augustus, with the supporters, a knight in armor or on the right, and a lion gules on the left.”
---
“I do not remember that any woman I have ever met has struck my imagination as Mlle. d’Esgrignon did,” said Émile Blondet, to whom contemporary literature is indebted for this history among other things. “Truth to tell, I was a boy, a mere child at the time, and perhaps my memory-pictures of her owe something of their vivid color to a boy’s natural turn for the marvelous.
“If I was playing with other children on the Parade, and she came to walk there with her nephew Victurnien, the sight of her in the distance thrilled me with very much the effect of galvanism on a dead body. Child as I was, I felt as though new life had been given me.
“Mlle. Armande had hair of tawny gold; there was a delicate fine down on her cheek, with a silver gleam upon it which I loved to catch, putting myself so that I could see the outlines of her face lit up by the daylight, and feel the fascination of those dreamy emerald eyes, which sent a flash of fire through me whenever they fell upon my face. I used to pretend to roll on the grass before her in our games, only to try to reach her little feet, and admire them on a closer view. The soft whiteness of her skin, her delicate features, the clearly cut lines of her forehead, the grace of her slender figure, took me with a sense of surprise, while as yet I did not know that her shape was graceful, nor her brows beautiful, nor the outline of her face a perfect oval. I admired as children pray at that age, without too clearly understanding why they pray. When my piercing gaze attracted her notice, when she asked me (in that musical voice of hers, with more volume in it, as it seemed to me, than all other voices), ‘What are you doing little one? Why do you look at me?’﻿—I used to come nearer and wriggle and bite my fingernails, and redden and say, ‘I do not know.’ And if she chanced to stroke my hair with her white hand, and ask me how old I was, I would run away and call from a distance, ‘Eleven!’

===Chinese===

“埃斯格里尼翁家的纹章是金底上两道红斜带，”他继续说，“九百年来纹章从未更易；最初是什么样，今天还是什么样。所以我们的铭言是‘Cil est nostre’（此即吾属），得自腓力·奥古斯都朝代的一次骑士比武大会；护兽是右首一尊金甲骑士，左首一头红狮。”
---
“我不记得平生遇见的哪个女人，曾像德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐那样打动过我的想象。”埃米尔·布隆代说——当代文学尚欠他这份情，这个故事便得自他的叙述。“说实话，我当时还是个男孩，一个小孩子，也许我对她的记忆画面之所以色彩鲜明，多少要归功于男孩子天生爱好奇异的脾性。
“如果我在阅兵场上和别的孩子玩耍，她带着侄儿维克图尼安来散步，远远望见她的身影，我就会浑身一震，那感觉恰似电流通过死尸。我虽是个孩子，却仿佛被注入了新的生命。
“阿尔芒德小姐有一头黄褐的金发；她的脸颊上覆着一层细细的茸毛，泛着银色的微光，我最爱捕捉那光泽——我会特意站到能借天光看清她脸庞轮廓的位置，去领略那双梦幻般的绿眼睛的魅力；那双眼睛每次落到我脸上，都会射出一道火光，穿透我的全身。做游戏时，我常假装在她面前的草地上打滚，只为能凑近够到她的小脚，细细端详。她肌肤的柔白，她五官的精致，她额头的清晰线条，她纤细身姿的优雅，都令我暗暗惊奇，而那时我还不知道什么叫身段优美，不知道她的眉目秀美，也不知道她的脸型是完美的鹅蛋形。我像那个年纪的孩子祈祷一般地仰慕着，并不十分明白自己为什么要祈祷。当我锐利的注视引起她的注意，当她用她那音乐般的嗓音问我——在我听来，那嗓音比所有别的声音都更浑厚——‘小家伙，你在干什么？为什么这样看我？’——我就会挨上前去，扭动身子，咬着指甲，涨红了脸说：‘我不知道。’倘若她碰巧用白皙的手抚一抚我的头发，问我几岁了，我就会跑开，远远地喊：‘11岁！’

===Original===

“Every princess and fairy of my visions, as I read the *Arabian Nights*, looked and walked like Mlle. d’Esgrignon; and afterwards, when my drawing-master gave me heads from the antique to copy, I noticed that their hair was braided like Mlle. d’Esgrignon’s. Still later, when the foolish fancies had vanished one by one, Mlle. Armande remained vaguely in my memory as a type; that Mlle. Armande for whom men made way respectfully, following the tall brown-robed figure with their eyes along the Parade and out of sight. Her exquisitely graceful form, the rounded curves sometimes revealed by a chance gust of wind, and always visible to my eyes in spite of the ample folds of stuff, revisited my young man’s dreams. Later yet, when I came to think seriously over certain mysteries of human thought, it seemed to me that the feeling of reverence was first inspired in me by something expressed in Mlle. d’Esgrignon’s face and bearing. The wonderful calm of her face, the suppressed passion in it, the dignity of her movements, the saintly life of duties fulfilled﻿—all this touched and awed me. Children are more susceptible than people imagine to the subtle influences of ideas; they never make game of real dignity; they feel the charm of real graciousness, and beauty attracts them, for childhood itself is beautiful, and there are mysterious ties between things of the same nature.
“Mlle. d’Esgrignon was one of my religions. To this day I can never climb the staircase of some old manor-house but my foolish imagination must needs picture Mlle. Armande standing there, like the spirit of feudalism. I can never read old chronicles but she appears before my eyes in the shape of some famous woman of old times; she is Agnès Sorel, Marie Touchet, Gabrielle; and I lend her all the love that was lost in her heart, all the love that she never expressed. The angel shape seen in glimpses through the haze of childish fancies visits me now sometimes across the mists of dreams.”
---
Keep this portrait in mind; it is a faithful picture and sketch of character. Mlle. d’Esgrignon is one of the most instructive figures in this story; she affords an example of the mischief that may be done by the purest goodness for lack of intelligence.

===Chinese===

“我读《一千零一夜》的时候，我幻想中的每一位公主和仙女，容貌和步态都像德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐；后来，绘画老师给我古代雕像的头像临摹，我又发现她们的头发也像德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐的那样编着辫子。再后来，那些痴想一个个消散了，阿尔芒德小姐却作为一个典型隐约留在我的记忆里；就是那位阿尔芒德小姐，男人们恭恭敬敬地为她让路，目送那高高的褐衣身影沿着阅兵场走远，直到看不见。她那无比优雅的身姿，那一阵偶然的风偶尔掀开的圆润曲线——尽管衣褶层层，在我眼中却始终看得见——又回到我青年时代的梦里。更晚一些，当我开始认真思索人类思想的某些奥秘时，我发觉，崇敬之情最初正是由德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐的容貌和举止中的某种东西唤起的。她脸上那种奇妙的宁静，那其中压抑着的热情，她举止的端庄，她履行天职的圣洁生活——这一切都令我感动，令我敬畏。孩子们对观念的微妙影响远比人们想象的敏感；他们从不嘲弄真正的尊严；他们感觉得到真正的优雅的魅力，美吸引着他们，因为童年本身就是美的，而同类事物之间自有神秘的纽带。
“德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐是我的一种宗教。直到今天，每当我登上某座古老庄园府邸的楼梯，我这痴愚的想象总要描绘出阿尔芒德小姐伫立在那里的身影，像封建制度的精灵。每当我读古老的编年史，她便化作古时某位名媛出现在我眼前；她是阿涅丝·索雷尔，是玛丽·图谢，是加布里埃尔；我把她心底失落的爱、她从未表露的爱，一概借给了她。那个透过童稚幻想的薄雾偶或一现的天使身影，如今有时还穿过梦的迷雾来造访我。”
---
请记住这幅肖像；这是一幅忠实的画像与性格素描。德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐是本书中最具教益的人物之一；她提供了一个例证，说明最纯粹的善良若缺乏智力，可能造成怎样的祸害。

===Original===

Two-thirds of the émigrés returned to France during 1804 and 1805, and almost every exile from the Marquis d’Esgrignon’s province came back to the land of his fathers. There were certainly defections. Men of good birth entered the service of Napoleon, and went into the army or held places at the Imperial court, and others made alliances with the upstart families. All those who cast in their lots with the Empire retrieved their fortunes and recovered their estates, thanks to the Emperor’s munificence; and these for the most part went to Paris and stayed there. But some eight or nine families still remained true to the proscribed noblesse and loyal to the fallen monarchy. The La Roche-Guyons, Nouastres, Verneuils, Castérans, Troisvilles, and the rest were some of them rich, some of them poor; but money, more or less, scarcely counted for anything among them. They took an antiquarian view of themselves; for them the age and preservation of the pedigree was the one all-important matter; precisely as, for an amateur, the weight of metal in a coin is a small matter in comparison with clean lettering, a flawless stamp, and high antiquity. Of these families, the Marquis d’Esgrignon was the acknowledged head. His house became their cenacle. There His Majesty, Emperor and King, was never anything but “M. de Bonaparte”; there “the King” meant Louis XVIII, then at Mittau; there the Department was still the Province, and the prefecture the intendance.
The Marquis was honored among them for his admirable behavior, his loyalty as a noble, his undaunted courage; even as he was respected throughout the town for his misfortunes, his fortitude, his steadfast adherence to his political convictions. The man so admirable in adversity was invested with all the majesty of ruined greatness. His chivalrous fair-mindedness was so well known, that litigants many a time had referred their disputes to him for arbitration. All gently bred Imperialists and the authorities themselves showed as much indulgence for his prejudices as respect for his personal character; but there was another and a large section of the new society which was destined to be known after the Restoration as the Liberal party; and these, with du Croisier as their unacknowledged head, laughed at an aristocratic oasis which nobody might enter without proof of irreproachable descent.

===Chinese===

1804年和1805年间，三分之二的流亡者回到了法国，德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵那一省的流亡者几乎全都回到了父辈的土地上。当然也有变节的。出身高贵的人进入拿破仑麾下效力，有的从军，有的在帝国宫廷中任职，另一些则与暴发户人家结亲。所有押注于帝国的人都恢复了家业，收回了领地，这要归功于皇帝的慷慨；这些人大多去了巴黎，并且留在了那里。可是仍有八九家人家忠于遭受放逐的贵族阶级，忠于倾覆的君主制度。拉罗什-居荣家、努瓦斯特家、韦尔讷伊家、卡斯特朗家、特鲁瓦维尔家等等，有的富裕，有的贫穷；但在他们中间，钱财的多寡几乎算不得什么。他们以古物鉴赏家的眼光看待自己；对他们来说，家谱的古老与完好才是唯一要紧的大事；正如对一位收藏家来说，钱币分量的轻重，比起铭文的清晰、币模的无瑕与年代的久远，不过是小事一桩。在这些家族中，德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵是公认的首领。他的府邸成了他们的圣地。在那里，皇帝兼国王陛下从来只是“德·波拿巴先生”；在那里，“国王”指的是当时身在米陶的路易十八；在那里，“郡”仍旧叫作“省”，省长公署仍旧叫作“总监府”。
侯爵在这些人家中备受尊崇，因为他品行高洁、贵族的忠义和无畏的胆识；正如他在全城受人敬重，是因为他的不幸、他的坚忍、他对政治信念的坚贞不渝。这位身处逆境仍如此可敬的人物，笼罩着没落了的伟业的全部威严。他骑士式的公正出了名，诉讼当事人常常把争端提请他仲裁。所有有教养的帝国派人士，连官府本身，对他的偏见所表现的宽容，不亚于对他个人品格的敬重；可是新社会中还有另一个庞大的阶层——复辟之后被称为自由党的那一派；这些人奉杜·克鲁瓦西耶为未公开承认的首领，讥笑那片贵族的绿洲——拿不出无可指摘的家世凭证，谁也别想踏进去。

===Original===

Their animosity was all the more bitter because honest country squires and the higher officials, with a good many worthy folk in the town, were of the opinion that all the best society thereof was to be found in the Marquis d’Esgrignon’s salon. The prefect himself, the Emperor’s chamberlain, made overtures to the d’Esgrignons, humbly sending his wife (a Grandlieu) as ambassadress.
Wherefore, those excluded from the miniature provincial Faubourg Saint-Germain nicknamed the salon “The Collection of Antiquities,” and called the Marquis himself “M. Carol.” The receiver of taxes, for instance, addressed his applications to “M. Carol (ci-devant des Grignons),” maliciously adopting the obsolete way of spelling.
---
“For my own part,” said Émile Blondet, “if I try to recall my childhood memories, I remember that the nickname of ‘Collection of Antiquities’ always made me laugh, in spite of my respect﻿—my love, I ought to say﻿—for Mlle. d’Esgrignon. The Hôtel d’Esgrignon stood at the angle of two of the busiest thoroughfares in the town, and not five hundred paces away from the market place. Two of the drawing-room windows looked upon the street and two upon the square; the room was like a glass cage, everyone who came past could look through it from side to side. I was only a boy of twelve at the time, but I thought, even then, that the salon was one of those rare curiosities which seem, when you come to think of them afterwards, to lie just on the borderland between reality and dreams, so that you can scarcely tell to which side they most belong.
“The room, the ancient Hall of Audience, stood above a row of cellars with grated air-holes, once the prison cells of the old courthouse, now converted into a kitchen. I do not know that the magnificent lofty chimneypiece of the Louvre, with its marvelous carving, seemed more wonderful to me than the vast open hearth of the salon d’Esgrignon when I saw it for the first time. It was covered like a melon with a network of tracery. Over it stood an equestrian portrait of Henri III, under whom the ancient duchy of appanage reverted to the crown; it was a great picture executed in low relief, and set in a carved and gilded frame. The ceiling spaces between the chestnut crossbeams in the fine old roof were decorated with scroll-work patterns; there was a little faded gilding still left along the angles.

===Chinese===

他们的敌意所以格外刻毒，是因为正直的乡绅、高级官员，连同城里许多可敬的人士，都认为全城最上流的社交圈子就在德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵的客厅里。连省长本人——皇帝的内廷侍从——也向德·埃斯格里尼翁家示好，谦恭地派他的妻子（一位格朗利厄家的小姐）充当女使节。
于是，那些进不了这个外省小型圣日耳曼区的人们，给这间客厅起了个绰号，叫“古物陈列室”，并把侯爵本人称作“卡罗尔先生”。比如，税务官递交申请时，抬头写的是“卡罗尔先生（旧姓德·格里尼翁）”，恶意地采用那种过时的拼法。
---
“就我自己的记忆而言，”埃米尔·布隆代说，“每当我回想童年的印象，我记得‘古物陈列室’这个绰号总能逗我发笑，尽管我敬重——我应该说是热爱——德·埃斯格里尼翁小姐。埃斯格里尼翁府坐落在城中两条最热闹的通衢交会的拐角上，离市场广场不到500步。客厅的窗户有两扇临街，两扇朝广场；那房间像一只玻璃笼子，过路人从这一面望进去，可以从那一面望出来。我那时才12岁，可即便当时我就觉得，这间客厅属于那种稀有的奇物——事后想来，它们恰好躺在现实与梦境的交界地带，你几乎说不清它们究竟更属于哪一边。
“这个房间，昔日的觐见厅，坐落在一排开着铁栅气窗的地窖之上；那些地窖从前是老法院的牢房，如今改作了厨房。我不敢说，卢浮宫那座雕工神妙的高大壁炉，初见之时在我眼里比埃斯格里尼翁客厅那巨大的敞式壁炉更令人惊叹。它像甜瓜一样覆着一层花格网架。壁炉上立着一幅亨利三世的骑马像——那块古老的采邑公国就是在他治下归并王室的；那是一幅浅浮雕式的大画，装在雕花镀金的画框里。老屋顶栗木横梁之间的天花板格档饰着涡卷花纹；棱角处还残留着一点褪色的镀金。

===Original===

The walls were covered with Flemish tapestry, six scenes from the Judgment of Solomon, framed in golden garlands, with satyrs and cupids playing among the leaves. The parquet floor had been laid down by the present Marquis, and Chesnel had picked up the furniture at sales of the wreckage of old châteaux between 1793 and 1795; so that there were Louis Quatorze consoles, tables, clock-cases, andirons, candle-sconces and tapestry-covered chairs, which marvelously completed a stately room, large out of all proportion to the house. Luckily, however, there was an equally lofty antechamber, the ancient Salle des Pas Perdus of the présidial, which communicated likewise with the magistrate’s deliberating chamber, used by the d’Esgrignons as a dining-room.
“Beneath the old paneling, amid the threadbare braveries of a bygone day, some eight or ten dowagers were drawn up in state in a quavering line; some with palsied heads, others dark and shriveled like mummies; some erect and stiff, others bowed and bent, but all of them tricked out in more or less fantastic costumes as far as possible removed from the fashion of the day, with be-ribboned caps above their curled and powdered ‘heads,’ and old discolored lace. No painter however earnest, no caricature however wild, ever caught the haunting fascination of those aged women; they come back to me in dreams; their puckered faces shape themselves in my memory whenever I meet an old woman who puts me in mind of them by some faint resemblance of dress or feature. And whether it is that misfortune has initiated me into the secrets of irremediable and overwhelming disaster; whether that I have come to understand the whole range of human feelings, and, best of all, the thoughts of Old Age and Regret; whatever the reason, nowhere and never again have I seen among the living or in the faces of the dying the wan look of certain gray eyes that I remember, nor the dreadful brightness of others that were black.
“Neither Hoffmann nor Maturin, the two weirdest imaginations of our time, ever gave me such a thrill of terror as I used to feel when I watched the automaton movements of those bodies sheathed in whalebone. The paint on actors’ faces never caused me a shock; I could see below it the rouge in grain, the *rouge de naissance*, to quote a comrade at least as malicious as I can be.

===Chinese===

四壁挂着弗拉芒壁毯，绘的是所罗门断案的六个场景，四周框着金色花环，林叶间有羊神和小爱神嬉戏。拼花地板是当今侯爵铺的；家具则是谢内尔在1793到1795年间从古堡劫余之物的拍卖中陆续捡来的：于是便有了路易十四时代的螺形托几、桌子、钟壳、柴架、壁灯和蒙着壁毯的椅子，它们奇妙地配成一间气派的厅堂，其宽敞与这所房子全然不成比例。所幸还有一间同样高敞的前厅，即从前大理院附属法院的“候审厅”，它还连通着法官合议室——德·埃斯格里尼翁家把它用作餐厅。
“旧壁板下面，在昔日荣华的破败排场中间，八九位孀居的老夫人庄重地列成一行颤抖的队伍；有的脑袋颤颤巍巍，有的黝黑干瘪像木乃伊；有的挺直僵硬，有的伛偻佝偻，但个个都装扮得或多或少古怪离奇，与当日的时尚相去天壤，缀着缎带的帽子罩住烫过、扑过粉的‘脑袋’，还有变了色的旧花边。再认真的画家，再放肆的漫画，也捕捉不到那些老妇人萦回不去的魅力；她们回到我的梦里；每当我遇见一位衣着或容貌依稀相像的老妇人，她们那些布满皱纹的面孔便在我记忆中浮现。不知是因为不幸使我领受了无可挽回的灭顶之灾的奥秘；还是因为我已参透人类情感的整个领域，尤其参透了老年与悔恨的心思；无论缘故何在，无论在生者中间，还是在弥留者的脸上，我再没有见过我所记得的那几双灰眼睛的枯槁神色，也没有见过另几双黑眼睛那种骇人的亮光。
“霍夫曼和马图林——我们时代两个最怪诞的想象力——也从不曾让我体验过那样的恐怖战栗，像我望着那些裹在鲸骨里的身躯做出木偶般的动作时所感到的那样。演员脸上的油彩从不曾使我震惊；我看得透那底下的天生红晕、生就的胭脂——借我一位起码与我一样刻薄的战友的话来说。

===Original===

Years had leveled those women’s faces, and at the same time furrowed them with wrinkles, till they looked like the heads on wooden nutcrackers carved in Germany. Peeping in through the windowpanes, I gazed at the battered bodies, and ill-jointed limbs (how they were fastened together, and, indeed, their whole anatomy was a mystery I never attempted to explain); I saw the lantern jaws, the protuberant bones, the abnormal development of the hips; and the movements of these figures as they came and went seemed to me no whit less extraordinary than their sepulchral immobility as they sat round the card-tables.
“The men looked gray and faded like the ancient tapestries on the wall, in dress they were much more like the men of the day, but even they were not altogether convincingly alive. Their white hair, their withered waxen-hued faces, their devastated foreheads and pale eyes, revealed their kinship to the women, and neutralized any effects of reality borrowed from their costume.
“The very certainty of finding all these folk seated at or among the tables every day at the same hours invested them at length in my eyes with a sort of spectacular interest as it were; there was something theatrical, something unearthly about them.
“Whenever, in after times, I have gone through museums of old furniture in Paris, London, Munich, or Vienna, with the gray-headed custodian who shows you the splendors of time past, I have peopled the rooms with figures from the Collection of Antiquities. Often, as little schoolboys of eight or ten we used to propose to go and take a look at the curiosities in their glass cage, for the fun of the thing. But as soon as I caught sight of Mlle. Armande’s sweet face, I used to tremble; and there was a trace of jealousy in my admiration for the lovely child Victurnien, who belonged, as we all instinctively felt, to a different and higher order of being from our own. It struck me as something indescribably strange that the young fresh creature should be there in that cemetery awakened before the time. We could not have explained our thoughts to ourselves, yet we felt that we were bourgeois and insignificant in the presence of that proud court.”

===Chinese===

岁月磨平了那些妇人的脸，同时又用皱纹在上面犁出沟壑，直到她们看上去像德国雕的木胡桃夹子上的脑袋。隔着窗玻璃窥望，我打量那些残损的身躯和接榫失灵的四肢（它们究竟如何连在一起，说实在的，她们的整个构造都是我从未想去解释的谜）；我看见那瘦削的凹颊，凸出的骨头，异样发达的胯部；这些身影往来走动时的举动，在我眼里丝毫不比她们围坐牌桌旁时坟墓般的凝滞更少离奇。
“男人们看上去灰暗褪色，像墙上古老的壁毯；论衣着，他们同当日的男人相像得多，但就连他们也不大像确凿的活人。他们的白发，他们蜡色的枯脸，他们荒芜的额头和苍白的眼睛，暴露出他们与那些妇人的亲缘，抵消了服装带给他们的任何真实感。
“每天在同样的钟点准能看见所有这些人坐到牌桌旁或牌桌之间，这种确凿无疑终于使他们在我的眼中蒙上了一种近乎看戏的兴味；他们身上有某种戏剧性的、非尘世的东西。
“后来，每当我在巴黎、伦敦、慕尼黑或维也纳的旧家具博物馆里，跟着把往昔的荣光指给你看的灰发看守人走过一间间展厅，我便让古物陈列室里的人物住满那些房间。常常，我们这些八九岁、十来岁的小学生会提议到玻璃笼子里去瞧瞧那些古董，图个乐子。可我一望见阿尔芒德小姐那张温柔的脸，就会发抖；我对漂亮孩子维克图尼安的仰慕中还掺着一丝妒意——我们全都本能地感到，他属于与我们不同、也比我们高贵的一等生命。那个年轻鲜嫩的生命竟然在那座提前苏醒的墓园里，这在我看来有种难以言喻的怪诞。我们说不清自己的想法，却感到在那高傲的宫廷面前，我们是资产者，是微不足道的。

===Original===

---
The disasters of 1813 and 1814, which brought about the downfall of Napoleon, gave new life to the Collection of Antiquities, and what was more than life, the hope of recovering their past importance; but the events of 1815, the troubles of the foreign occupation, and the vacillating policy of the Government until the fall of M. Decazes, all contributed to defer the fulfilment of the expectations of the personages so vividly described by Blondet. This story, therefore, only begins to shape itself in 1822.
In 1822 the Marquis d’Esgrignon’s fortunes had not improved in spite of the changes worked by the Restoration in the condition of émigrés. Of all nobles hardly hit by Revolutionary legislation, his case was the hardest. Like other great families, the d’Esgrignons before 1789 derived the greater part of their income from their rights as lords of the manor in the shape of dues paid by those who held of them; and, naturally, the old seigneurs had reduced the size of the holdings in order to swell the amounts paid in quit-rents and heriots. Families in this position were hopelessly ruined. They were not affected by the ordinance by which Louis XVIII put the émigrés into possession of such of their lands as had not been sold; and at a later date it was impossible that the law of indemnity should indemnify them. Their suppressed rights, as everybody knows, were revived in the shape of a land tax known by the very name of *domaines*, but the money went into the coffers of the State.
The Marquis by his position belonged to that small section of the Royalist party which would hear of no kind of compromise with those whom they styled, not Revolutionaries, but revolted subjects, or, in more parliamentary language, they had no dealings with Liberals or Constitutionnels. Such Royalists, nicknamed Ultras by the opposition, took for leaders and heroes those courageous orators of the Right, who from the very beginning attempted, with M. de Polignac, to protest against the charter granted by Louis XVIII. This they regarded as an ill-advised edict extorted from the Crown by the necessity of the moment, only to be annulled later on.

===Chinese===

---
1813年和1814年的灾难导致了拿破仑的垮台，给古物陈列室注入了新的生命，而且不止是生命，还有恢复昔日地位的希望；可是1815年的变故、外国占领的动荡，以及直到德卡泽先生倒台前政府摇摆不定的政策，都使布隆代描绘得如此鲜明的人物的期待迟迟不能实现。因此，这个故事到1822年才真正成形。
到了1822年，尽管复辟改变了流亡者的境遇，德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵的家道并未好转。在深受革命立法打击的贵族中，他的处境最为艰难。同别的世家一样，1789年以前，德·埃斯格里尼翁家的主要收入来自领主权利，即耕种他们土地的佃户所缴的种种捐税；而且，老领主们为了增加地租和继承金的收入，自然缩小了佃户持有地的面积。处于这种境地的家族是无可救药地破产了。路易十八将未售出的土地发还给流亡者的敕令，对他们并无助益；日后的赔偿法也不可能赔偿他们。众所周知，他们那些被废除的权利后来以一项土地税的形式复活了，税名就叫domaines（领地税），可是钱进了国库。
以侯爵的立场，他属于保王党中央那个小小的派别：这个派别绝不同那些人妥协——他们不称那些人为革命者，而称之为叛乱的臣民；或者用更议会式的语言说，他们与自由党人、宪政党人绝无往来。这类保王党人被反对派起了个绰号叫“极端派”，他们奉右派那些勇敢的演说家为领袖和英雄；这些人从一开始就同德·波利尼亚克先生一道，试图抗议路易十八颁布的宪章。他们把宪章看作时势所迫、从王冠头上勒索来的一道失策敕令，日后只应废除。

===Original===

And, therefore, so far from cooperating with the King to bring about a new condition of things, the Marquis d’Esgrignon stood aloof, an upholder of the straitest sect of the Right in politics, until such time as his vast fortune should be restored to him. Nor did he so much as admit the thought of the indemnity which filled the minds of the Villèle ministry, and formed a part of a design of strengthening the Crown by putting an end to those fatal distinctions of ownership which still lingered on in spite of legislation.
The miracles of the Restoration of 1814, the still greater miracle of Napoleon’s return in 1815, the portents of a second flight of the Bourbons, and a second reinstatement (that almost fabulous phase of contemporary history), all these things took the Marquis by surprise at the age of sixty-seven. At that time of life, the most high-spirited men of their age were not so much vanquished as worn out in the struggle with the Revolution; their activity, in their remote provincial retreats, had turned into a passionately held and immovable conviction; and almost all of them were shut in by the enervating, easy round of daily life in the country. Could worse luck befall a political party than this﻿—to be represented by old men at a time when its ideas are already stigmatized as old-fashioned?
When the legitimate sovereign appeared to be firmly seated on the throne again in 1818, the Marquis asked himself what a man of seventy should do at court; and what duties, what office he could discharge there? The noble and high-minded d’Esgrignon was fain to be content with the triumph of the Monarchy and Religion, while he waited for the results of that unhoped-for, indecisive victory, which proved to be simply an armistice. He continued as before, lord-paramount of his salon, so felicitously named the Collection of Antiquities.
But when the victors of 1793 became the vanquished in their turn, the nickname given at first in jest began to be used in bitter earnest. The town was no more free than other country towns from the hatreds and jealousies bred of party spirit.

===Chinese===

因此，德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵非但不与国王合作开创新的局面，反而置身局外，做政治上最严格的右派宗义的守护者，直到他那份巨额家产归还于他。赔偿的念头——它占满了维莱尔内阁的心思，是那个旨在消灭虽经立法却依旧残留的所有权致命差别、从而巩固王权的方案的一部分——他连想也不肯想。
1814年复辟的奇迹，1815年拿破仑返回的更大的奇迹，波旁王室第二次出逃的凶兆，以及第二次复位（当代史上近乎传奇的一段）——这一切在侯爵67岁上突如其来，令他措手不及。到了这个年纪，那一代人中最血气方刚的，与其说是在同革命的搏斗中被击败，不如说是被拖垮了；他们在偏僻的外省隐居地里的活动，已经化作一种狂热坚持、不可动摇的信念；而且他们几乎全都被乡间消磨人的安逸日常禁锢住了。一个政党还能遭逢比这更坏的运气吗——当它的主张已被贬为陈旧过时的时候，代表它的却是一群老人？
1818年，合法的君王似乎重新在王座上坐稳了，侯爵自问：一个70岁的人到宫廷里去做什么？在那里又能履行什么职责、担当什么官职？高贵而胸襟坦荡的德·埃斯格里尼翁，只好满足于君主制与宗教的胜利，一面等待那场出乎意料而又胜负未定的胜利的结果——后来证明，那不过是一次休战。他依旧一如既往，做他客厅里的至尊领主；那客厅得了个再贴切不过的名字：古物陈列室。
可是，当1793年的胜利者轮到自己变成失败者时，当初出于玩笑的绰号开始被狠狠地当真使用起来。这座小城同别的外省城市一样，免不了党派精神所滋生的仇恨与嫉妒。

===Original===

Du Croisier, contrary to all expectation, married the old maid who had refused him at first; carrying her off from his rival, the darling of the aristocratic quarter, a certain Chevalier whose illustrious name will be sufficiently hidden by suppressing it altogether, in accordance with the usage formerly adopted in the place itself, where he was known by his title only. He was “the Chevalier” in the town, as the Comte d’Artois was “Monsieur” at court. Now, not only had that marriage produced a war after the provincial manner, in which all weapons are fair; it had hastened the separation of the great and little noblesse, of the aristocratic and bourgeois social elements, which had been united for a little space by the heavy weight of Napoleonic rule. After the pressure was removed, there followed that sudden revival of class divisions which did so much harm to the country.
The most national of all sentiments in France is vanity. The wounded vanity of the many induced a thirst for Equality; though, as the most ardent innovator will some day discover, Equality is an impossibility. The Royalists pricked the Liberals in the most sensitive spots, and this happened specially in the provinces, where either party accused the other of unspeakable atrocities. In those days the blackest deeds were done in politics, to secure public opinion on one side or the other, to catch the votes of that public of fools which holds up hands for those that are clever enough to serve out weapons to them. Individuals are identified with their political opinions, and opponents in public life forthwith became private enemies. It is very difficult in a country town to avoid a man-to-man conflict of this kind over interests or questions which in Paris appear in a more general and theoretical form, with the result that political combatants also rise to a higher level; M. Laffitte, for example, or M. Casimir-Périer can respect M. de Villèle or M. de Payronnet as a man. M. Laffitte, who drew the fire on the Ministry, would have given them an asylum in his house if they had fled thither on the 29th of July 1830. Benjamin Constant sent a copy of his work on Religion to the Vicomte de Chateaubriand, with a flattering letter acknowledging benefits received from the former Minister.

===Chinese===

出乎一切意料，杜·克鲁瓦西耶娶了当初拒绝过他的那位老姑娘，是从他情敌手里把她夺来的。那情敌是贵族区的宠儿，某位骑士——他的显赫姓氏，还是照当地旧日的惯例索性略去为妙，因为在城里大家只以他的头衔相称。他在城里是“骑士先生”，正如阿图瓦伯爵在宫廷里是“殿下”。要知道，那桩婚事不但引起了一场外省式的战争——在那样的战争里一切武器都是合法的；它还加速了大贵族与小贵族、贵族社交界与资产阶级社交界的分离，拿破仑统治的重压曾使这两者短暂地联合；压力一旦解除，接踵而来的便是阶级分裂的突然复活，给国家造成了那么大的祸害。
法兰西最具民族性的情感是虚荣。多数人受了伤害的虚荣心激起对平等的渴望；尽管如同最热忱的革新者终有一天会发现的那样，平等是不可能实现的。保王党人专戳自由党人最敏感的痛处，这在外省尤其如此，在那里双方互相指控对方犯下说不出口的暴行。那些日子里，政治上最黑暗的勾当层出不穷，为的是把舆论争取到自己一边，抓住那群傻瓜公众的选票——他们专门替那些聪明得懂得给他们发放武器的家伙举手。个人与政见混为一体，公共生活中的对手随即变成私人的仇敌。在外省小城，很难避免这种围绕利益或问题的逐人相斗；这些问题在巴黎是以更笼统、更理论的形式出现的，于是政治斗士的水准也随之提高：比如拉菲特先生或卡西米尔-佩里耶先生，能够尊重维莱尔先生或佩罗内先生这个人。拉菲特先生曾把火力引向内阁，可如果1830年7月29日内阁成员逃到他家里避难，他也会收留他们的。邦雅曼·贡斯当把自己的宗教论著寄给德·夏多布里昂子爵，还附了一封表示感恩的奉承信。

===Original===

At Paris men are systems, whereas in the provinces systems are identified with men; men, moreover, with restless passions, who must always confront one another, always spy upon each other in private life, and pull their opponents’ speeches to pieces, and live generally like two duelists on the watch for a chance to thrust six inches of steel between an antagonist’s ribs. Each must do his best to get under his enemy’s guard, and a political hatred becomes as all-absorbing as a duel to the death. Epigram and slander are used against individuals to bring the party into discredit.
In such warfare as this, waged ceremoniously and without rancor on the side of the Antiquities, while du Croisier’s faction went so far as to use the poisoned weapons of savages﻿—in this warfare the advantages of wit and delicate irony lay on the side of the nobles. But it should never be forgotten that the wounds made by the tongue and the eyes, by gibe or slight, are the last of all to heal. When the Chevalier turned his back on mixed society and entrenched himself on the Mons Sacer of the aristocracy, his witticisms thenceforward were directed at du Croisier’s salon; he stirred up the fires of war, not knowing how far the spirit of revenge was to urge the rival faction. None but purists and loyal gentlemen and women sure one of another entered the Hôtel d’Esgrignon; they committed no indiscretions of any kind; they had their ideas, true or false, good or bad, noble or trivial, but there was nothing to laugh at in all this. If the Liberals meant to make the nobles ridiculous, they were obliged to fasten on the political actions of their opponents; while the intermediate party, composed of officials and others who paid court to the higher powers, kept the nobles informed of all that was done and said in the Liberal camp, and much of it was abundantly laughable. Du Croisier’s adherents smarted under a sense of inferiority, which increased their thirst for revenge.
In 1822, du Croisier put himself at the head of the manufacturing interest of the province, as the Marquis d’Esgrignon headed the noblesse. Each represented his party. But du Croisier, instead of giving himself out frankly for a man of the extreme Left, ostensibly adopted the opinions formulated at a later date by the 221 deputies.

===Chinese===

在巴黎，人是主义的化身；而在外省，主义却与人合为一体；何况这些人满腔躁动的激情，必须时时正面相抗，在私生活中互相窥伺，把对手的言论拆得支离破碎；总之活得像两个决斗者，时刻窥伺着把6寸钢刃捅进仇敌肋间的机会。人人都想骗过对手的剑防，于是政治仇恨变得像生死决斗一样吞噬一切。人们用俏皮话和诽谤攻击个人，借以败坏整个党派的名声。
在这样的战争中——古物陈列室一方打得彬彬有礼、不怀怨毒，而杜·克鲁瓦西耶一派竟至用起野人的毒箭——在这样的战争中，机智与精妙讽刺的优势在贵族一边。但永远不该忘记，舌头和眼睛造成的创伤，讥诮与慢待造成的创伤，是最难愈合的。当骑士先生背弃了混杂的社交界，退守到贵族的“圣山”之后，他的妙语从此便都射向杜·克鲁瓦西耶的客厅；他挑旺战火，却不知道复仇之心会把敌对的一派驱赶到哪一步。踏进埃斯格里尼翁府的，只有纯粹派和彼此深信不疑的忠贞绅士淑女；他们不干任何轻率失检的事；他们自有主张，或对或错，或好或坏，或高贵或琐屑，但这一切毫无可笑之处。自由党人若想把贵族变成笑柄，便只能抓住对方的政治行动不放；而由官员和向当局献殷勤的人组成的中间派，则把自由党营垒里的一切言行通报给贵族，其中许多实在可笑之至。杜·克鲁瓦西耶的信徒们因自愧弗如而心怀刺痛，这更加剧了他们复仇的渴望。
1822年，杜·克鲁瓦西耶出面领导本省的实业界，正如德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵领导贵族。两人各为一党的代表。可是杜·克鲁瓦西耶并不坦率承认自己是极左派，表面上只接受了后来由221名议员表述的那些政见。

===Original===

By taking up this position, he could keep in touch with the magistrates and local officials and the capitalists of the department. Du Croisier’s salon, a power at least equal to the salon d’Esgrignon, larger numerically, as well as younger and more energetic, made itself felt all over the countryside; the Collection of Antiquities, on the other hand, remained inert, a passive appendage, as it were, of a central authority which was often embarrassed by its own partisans; for not merely did they encourage the Government in a mistaken policy, but some of its most fatal blunders were made in consequence of the pressure brought to bear upon it by the Conservative party.
The Liberals, so far, had never contrived to carry their candidate. The department declined to obey their command, knowing that du Croisier, if elected, would take his place on the Left Centre benches, and as far as possible to the Left. Du Croisier was in correspondence with the Brothers Keller, the bankers, the oldest of whom shone conspicuous among “the nineteen deputies of the Left,” that phalanx made famous by the efforts of the entire Liberal press. This same M. Keller, moreover, was related by marriage to the Comte de Gondreville, a Constitutional peer who remained in favor with Louis XVIII. For these reasons, the Constitutional Opposition (as distinct from the Liberal party) was always prepared to vote at the last moment, not for the candidate whom they professed to support, but for du Croisier, if that worthy could succeed in gaining a sufficient number of Royalist votes; but at every election du Croisier was regularly thrown out by the Royalists. The leaders of that party, taking their tone from the Marquis d’Esgrignon, had pretty thoroughly fathomed and gauged their man; and with each defeat, du Croisier and his party waxed more bitter. Nothing so effectually stirs up strife as the failure of some snare set with elaborate pains.
In 1822 there seemed to be a lull in hostilities which had been kept up with great spirit during the first four years of the Restoration. The salon du Croisier and the salon d’Esgrignon, having measured their strength and weakness, were in all probability waiting for opportunity, that Providence of party strife.

===Chinese===

占据这个位置，他便可以同法官、地方官员和本郡的资本家保持联络。杜·克鲁瓦西耶的客厅，势力至少与埃斯格里尼翁客厅相当，人数更多，也更年轻，更有干劲，其影响遍及整个乡野；古物陈列室则相反，始终无所作为，宛如中央政权的一个被动的附属物——而这个政权时常被自己的拥护者弄得窘迫；因为这些人不但怂恿政府执行错误的政策，而且政府某些最致命的失策，正是保守党施加压力的结果。
迄今为止，自由党人从未能让自己提出的候选人当选。全郡拒不服从他们的号令，因为大家知道，杜·克鲁瓦西耶一旦当选，便会坐上中左派的议席，而且要尽量坐向左面。杜·克鲁瓦西耶与银行家凯勒兄弟通信，兄长在“左派19议员”中间名声显赫，那支方阵靠整个自由党报界的鼓噪出了名。而且正是这位凯勒先生，与德·贡德维尔伯爵联了姻，那位宪政派贵族院议员始终得路易十八的欢心。由于这些缘故，宪政派反对党（与自由党有别）随时准备在最后一刻不投他们口头上支持的候选人，而投杜·克鲁瓦西耶的票——只要这位仁兄能争取到足够数目的保王党选票；可是每逢选举，杜·克鲁瓦西耶照例被保王党人拉下马来。该党的首领们以德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵的调子为准，早已把此人揣摩得透透彻彻；而每败一次，杜·克鲁瓦西耶和他的党徒便怨毒更深一层。再没有什么比精心设下的罗网落空更能挑起争斗的了。
1822年，复辟最初四年间打得热火朝天的战事似乎出现了沉寂。杜·克鲁瓦西耶客厅与埃斯格里尼翁客厅既然已经较量过彼此的强弱，多半是在等待时机——那党派之争的天意。

===Original===

Ordinary persons were content with the surface quiet which deceived the Government; but those who knew du Croisier better, were well aware that the passion of revenge in him, as in all men whose whole life consists in mental activity, is implacable, especially when political ambitions are involved. About this time du Croisier, who used to turn white and red at the bare mention of d’Esgrignon or the Chevalier, and shuddered at the name of the Collection of Antiquities, chose to wear the impassive countenance of a savage. He smiled upon his enemies, hating them but the more deeply, watching them the more narrowly from hour to hour. One of his own party, who seconded him in these calculations of cold wrath, was the President of the Tribunal, M. du Ronceret, a little country squire, who had vainly endeavored to gain admittance among the Antiquities.
The d’Esgrignons’ little fortune, carefully administered by Maître Chesnel, was barely sufficient for the worthy Marquis’ needs; for though he lived without the slightest ostentation, he also lived like a noble. The governor found by his Lordship the Bishop for the hope of the house, the young Comte Victurnien d’Esgrignon, was an elderly Oratorian who must be paid a certain salary, although he lived with the family. The wages of a cook, a waiting-woman for Mlle. Armande, an old valet for M. le Marquis, and a couple of other servants, together with the daily expenses of the household, and the cost of an education for which nothing was spared, absorbed the whole family income, in spite of Mlle. Armande’s economies, in spite of Chesnel’s careful management, and the servants’ affection. As yet, Chesnel had not been able to set about repairs at the ruined castle; he was waiting till the leases fell in to raise the rent of the farms, for rents had been rising lately, partly on account of improved methods of agriculture, partly by the fall in the value of money, of which the landlord would get the benefit at the expiration of leases granted in 1809.
The Marquis himself knew nothing of the details of the management of the house or of his property. He would have been thunderstruck if he had been told of the excessive precautions needed “to make both ends of the year meet in December,” to use the housewife’s saying, and he was so near the end of his life, that everyone shrank from opening his eyes.

===Chinese===

寻常人满足于那骗过了政府的表面平静；而深知杜·克鲁瓦西耶为人的人心里明白，他身上的复仇欲，同所有全靠心智活动过活的人一样，是不共戴天的，尤其当政治野心牵涉在内的时候。大约在这时候，杜·克鲁瓦西耶——从前只要有人一提德·埃斯格里尼翁或骑士先生的名字，他便会红一阵白一阵，一听见“古物陈列室”便浑身发抖——偏偏摆出一副野蛮人式的无动于衷的面孔。他对敌人微笑，恨得却更深，时时刻刻盯他们盯得更紧。他一党之中有一个协助他进行这些冷怒盘算的人，便是法院院长迪·龙瑟雷先生，一个小乡绅，他曾枉费心机想挤进古物陈列室。
德·埃斯格里尼翁家微薄的家产，经谢内尔师傅的悉心打理，也仅够这位可敬的侯爵开销；因为他虽然绝无半点铺张，到底过着贵族的日子。主教大人为全家的希望——年轻的维克图尼安·德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵——物色的家庭教师，是一位年长的奥拉托利会会士，虽与主人家同住，却须付他一份薪水。一名厨娘、阿尔芒德小姐的一名贴身女仆、侯爵先生的一名老仆，连同另外两三个仆人的工钱，再加上日常家用，以及不惜工本的教育费用，把全家收入吞没殆尽，任凭阿尔芒德小姐节俭，任凭谢内尔精打细算，任凭仆人们一片忠心。直到如今，谢内尔还没能着手修缮那座残破的城堡；他在等租约到期好提高田庄的租金，因为近来租金一直在上涨，一部分由于耕作方法的改良，一部分由于货币的贬值——这些租约都是1809年订立的，到期之后，好处自然落到地主手里。
侯爵本人对家务或产业经营的细节一概不知。倘若有人告诉他，照主妇们的说法，要让“年头的这一端和那一端在十二月对上账”，需要何等过度的防范，他会惊得目瞪口呆；而他离生命的尽头已如此之近，谁都不忍让他睁开眼睛。

===Original===

The Marquis and his adherents believed that a House, to which no one at Court or in the Government gave a thought, a House that was never heard of beyond the gates of the town, save here and there in the same department, was about to revive its ancient greatness, to shine forth in all its glory. The d’Esgrignons’ line should appear with renewed lustre in the person of Victurnien, just as the despoiled nobles came into their own again, and the handsome heir to a great estate would be in a position to go to Court, enter the King’s service, and marry (as other d’Esgrignons had done before him) a Navarreins, a Cadignan, a d’Uxelles, a Beauséant, a Blamont-Chauvry; a wife, in short, who should unite all the distinctions of birth and beauty, wit and wealth, and character.
The intimates who came to play their game of cards of an evening﻿—the Troisvilles (pronounced Tréville), the La Roche-Guyons, the Castérans (pronounced Catéran), and the Duc de Verneuil﻿—had all so long been accustomed to look up to the Marquis as a person of immense consequence, that they encouraged him in such notions as these. They were perfectly sincere in their belief; and indeed, it would have been well founded if they could have wiped out the history of the last forty years. But the most honorable and undoubted sanctions of right, such as Louis XVIII had tried to set on record when he dated the Charter from the one-and-twentieth year of his reign, only exist when ratified by the general consent. The d’Esgrignons not only lacked the very rudiments of the language of latter-day politics, to wit, money, the great modern *relief*, or sufficient rehabilitation of nobility; but, in their case, too, “historical continuity” was lacking, and that is a kind of renown which tells quite as much at Court as on the battlefield, in diplomatic circles as in Parliament, with a book, or in connection with an adventure; it is, as it were, a sacred ampulla poured upon the heads of each successive generation. Whereas a noble family, inactive and forgotten, is very much in the position of a hard-featured, poverty-stricken, simple-minded, and virtuous maid, these qualifications being the four cardinal points of misfortune.

===Chinese===

侯爵和他的党徒们相信，这个在宫廷里、在政府中无人挂念的家族，这个出了城门便无人听闻——至多在本郡偶有所闻——的家族，即将复兴古老的荣光，即将大放异彩。德·埃斯格里尼翁家的血脉将在维克图尼安身上重焕光辉，正如被剥夺的贵族重新得回自己的一切；这位俊美的巨额遗产继承人将有资本进宫廷，为国王效力，并且（像他之前的历代埃斯格里尼翁那样）娶一位纳夫兰家、卡迪尼昂家、德·于克塞尔家、博塞昂家或布拉蒙-绍弗里家的小姐；总之，娶一位集门第与美貌、才智与财富、品格于一身的妻子。
晚间来打牌的至交——特鲁瓦维尔家（读作特雷维尔）、拉罗什-居荣家、卡斯特朗家（读作卡泰朗），还有德·韦尔讷伊公爵——长久以来惯于把侯爵奉为响当当的大人物，便一味助长他这样的念头。他们的信念完全真诚；而且，倘若他们能把过去40年的历史一笔抹掉，这信念倒也不无根据。可是，即便是最体面、最无可置疑的权利认可——比如路易十八把宪章的日期署为自己在位第21年时试图载明的那些——也唯有得到普遍的同意才能存在。德·埃斯格里尼翁家不仅缺少当日政治语言最基本的要素，也就是金钱——现代的大救星——或对贵族身份的充分恢复；而且他们还缺少“历史延续性”；那是一种声望，在宫廷里与在战场上、在外交界与在议会里、连同一部书或一桩奇遇，同样举足轻重；它仿佛是一勺圣膏，浇在一辈又一辈人的头顶。而一个无所作为、被人遗忘的贵族家族，处境便很像一个容貌丑陋、一贫如洗、头脑简单而品行端正的姑娘——这四种资质正是厄运的四个基点。

===Original===

The marriage of a daughter of the Troisvilles with General Montcornet, so far from opening the eyes of the Antiquities, very nearly brought about a rupture between the Troisvilles and the salon d’Esgrignon, the latter declaring that the Troisvilles were mixing themselves up with all sorts of people.
There was one, and one only, among all these folk who did not share their illusions. And that one, needless to say, was Chesnel the notary. Although his devotion, sufficiently proved already, was simply unbounded for the great house now reduced to three persons; although he accepted all their ideas, and thought them nothing less than right, he had too much common sense, he was too good a man of business to more than half the families in the department, to miss the significance of the great changes that were taking place in people’s minds, or to be blind to the different conditions brought about by industrial development and modern manners. He had watched the Revolution pass through the violent phase of 1793, when men, women, and children wore arms, and heads fell on the scaffold, and victories were won in pitched battles with Europe; and now he saw the same forces quietly at work in men’s minds, in the shape of ideas which sanctioned the issues. The soil had been cleared, the seed sown, and now came the harvest. To his thinking, the Revolution had formed the mind of the younger generation; he touched the hard facts, and knew that although there were countless unhealed wounds, what had been done was past recall. The death of a king on the scaffold, the protracted agony of a queen, the division of the nobles’ lands, in his eyes were so many binding contracts; and where so many vested interests were involved, it was not likely that those concerned would allow them to be attacked. Chesnel saw clearly. His fanatical attachment to the d’Esgrignons was wholehearted, but it was not blind, and it was all the fairer for this. The young monk’s faith that sees heaven laid open and beholds the angels, is something far below the power of the old monk who points them out to him. The ex-steward was like the old monk; he would have given his life to defend a worm-eaten shrine.

===Chinese===

特鲁瓦维尔家的一位小姐嫁给蒙科内将军，这件事非但没有睁开古物陈列室诸公的眼睛，反倒几乎造成特鲁瓦维尔家与埃斯格里尼翁客厅的决裂，后者声称特鲁瓦维尔家什么人都混在一起交往。
这些人当中只有一个，而且仅有那么一个，不与他们共享幻想。不必说，那便是公证人谢内尔。虽然他的忠心早已屡经验证，对这个如今只剩三个人的名门可谓无穷无尽；虽然他接受他们的一切观念，认为它们天经地义，但他有太多的常识，他是连本郡过半数人家都比不上的精明生意人，不会看不出人心正在发生的巨大变化意味着什么，也不会对工业发展和现代风气造成的种种新境况视而不见。他目睹革命度过了1793年那个暴烈的阶段，那时男人、女人和孩子都带着武器，人头滚落断头台，与全欧洲的会战中赢得胜利；如今他又看见同样的力量以观念的形态在人们心中悄然运行，这些观念为既成的结果正名。土地已经清整，种子已经播下，如今收获的季节到了。在他看来，革命塑造了年轻一代的头脑；他触到了坚硬的事实，知道尽管有无数未曾愈合的创口，既成之事已无可挽回。一位国王死在断头台上，一位王后久受煎熬，贵族土地的分割，在他眼里是一张张有约束力的契约；既然牵涉到这么多既得利益，利益相关的人绝不会容许别人攻击它们。谢内尔看得分明。他对德·埃斯格里尼翁家的狂热依恋全心全意，却并不盲目，正因如此才愈发可贵。年轻修士的信仰能看见洞开的天堂、瞻仰天使，却远不及指点他仰望的老修士的功力。昔日的大管家正是那老修士；为捍卫一座虫蛀的神龛，他甘愿献出生命。

===Original===

He tried to explain the “innovations” to his old master, using a thousand tactful precautions; sometimes speaking jestingly, sometimes affecting surprise or sorrow over this or that; but he always met the same prophetic smile on the Marquis’ lips, the same fixed conviction in the Marquis’ mind, that these follies would go by like others. Events contributed in a way which has escaped attention to assist such noble champions of forlorn hope to cling to their superstitions. What could Chesnel do when the old Marquis said, with a lordly gesture, “God swept away Bonaparte with his armies, his new great vassals, his crowned kings, and his vast conceptions! God will deliver us from the rest.” And Chesnel hung his head sadly, and did not dare to answer, “It cannot be God’s will to sweep away France.” Yet both of them were grand figures; the one, standing out against the torrent of facts like an ancient block of lichen-covered granite, still upright in the depths of an Alpine gorge; the other, watching the course of the flood to turn it to account. Then the good gray-headed notary would groan over the irreparable havoc which the superstitions were sure to work in the mind, the habits, and ideas of the Comte Victurnien d’Esgrignon.
Idolized by his father, idolized by his aunt, the young heir was a spoilt child in every sense of the word; but still a spoilt child who justified paternal and maternal illusions. Maternal, be it said, for Victurnien’s aunt was truly a mother to him; and yet, however careful and tender she may be that never bore a child, there is something lacking in her motherhood. A mother’s second sight cannot be acquired. An aunt, bound to her nursling by ties of such pure affection as united Mlle. Armande to Victurnien, may love as much as a mother might; may be as careful, as kind, as tender, as indulgent, but she lacks the mother’s instinctive knowledge when and how to be severe; she has no sudden warnings, none of the uneasy presentiments of the mother’s heart; for a mother, bound to her child from the beginnings of life by all the fibres of her being, still is conscious of the communication, still vibrates with the shock of every trouble, and thrills with every joy in the child’s life as if it were her own.

===Chinese===

他试着向老主人解释这些“革新”，用尽了千般委婉的小心；有时说得像玩笑，有时又对这事那事佯装惊讶或惋惜；可他总在侯爵唇边撞见同样的先知般的微笑，在侯爵心里撞见同样的执拗信念——这些荒唐事会同别的荒唐事一样过去。时事还以一种无人留意的方式，帮着这些绝望事业的高贵斗士们死抱住他们的迷信不放。老侯爵做着一个倨傲的手势说：“上帝扫荡了波拿巴，连同他的军队、他的新兴大诸侯、他的加冕国王们和他那宏大的构想！上帝也会把我们从其余的一切里解救出来。”谢内尔还能有什么办法呢？谢内尔悲哀地垂下头，不敢回答：“把法兰西扫荡掉，不可能是上帝的旨意。”然而他们两个都是堂堂的人物：一个像阿尔卑斯峡谷深处一块覆满地衣的古老花岗岩，依然挺立，迎着事实的洪流；另一个观察洪水的走向，好加以利用。随后，善良的白发公证人便会悲叹：这些迷信必定会在维克图尼安·德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵的头脑、习惯和观念中造成无可挽回的浩劫。
年轻的继承人被父亲宠着，被姑母宠着，是个不折不扣被惯坏的孩子；不过这被惯坏的孩子倒也配得上父亲和母亲式的幻想。说是母亲式的，因为维克图尼安的姑母于他实在是一位母亲；然而，一个从未生育过的女人，无论她怎样细心、怎样温柔，她的母性里总缺少点什么。母亲的直觉是学不来的。一位姑母，同她的乳养对象之间有着如阿尔芒德小姐与维克图尼安之间那样纯洁的情感纽带，可以爱得像母亲一样深；可以一样细心，一样慈祥，一样温柔，一样纵容，但她缺少母亲那种何时严厉、如何严厉的本能的知；她没有不祥的预感，没有母亲心里那种惴惴不安的预兆；因为母亲从生命之初起，便以她整个存在的纤维与孩子相连，始终感觉得到这份相通，孩子生活中每一次磨难都令她震颤，每一分欢乐都令她激动，仿佛那就是她自己的。

===Original===

If Nature has made of woman, physically speaking, a neutral ground, it has not been forbidden to her, under certain conditions, to identify herself completely with her offspring. When she has not merely given life, but given of her whole life, you behold that wonderful, unexplained, and inexplicable thing﻿—the love of a woman for one of her children above the others. The outcome of this story is one more proof of a proven truth﻿—a mother’s place cannot be filled. A mother foresees danger long before a Mlle. Armande can admit the possibility of it, even if the mischief is done. The one prevents the evil, the other remedies it. And besides, in the maiden’s motherhood there is an element of blind adoration, she cannot bring herself to scold a beautiful boy.
A practical knowledge of life, and the experience of business, had taught the old notary a habit of distrustful clear-sighted observation something akin to the mother’s instinct. But Chesnel counted for so little in the house (especially since he had fallen into something like disgrace over that unlucky project of a marriage between a d’Esgrignon and a du Croisier), that he had made up his mind to adhere blindly in future to the family doctrines. He was a common soldier, faithful to his post, and ready to give his life; it was never likely that they would take his advice, even in the height of the storm; unless chance should bring him, like the King’s bedesman in *The Antiquary*, to the edge of the sea, when the old baronet and his daughter were caught by the high tide.
Du Croisier caught a glimpse of his revenge in the anomalous education given to the lad. He hoped, to quote the expressive words of the author quoted above, “to drown the lamb in its mother’s milk.” *This* was the hope which had produced his taciturn resignation and brought that savage smile on his lips.
The young Comte Victurnien was taught to believe in his own supremacy as soon as an idea could enter his head. All the great nobles of the realm were his peers, his one superior was the King, and the rest of mankind were his inferiors, people with whom he had nothing in common, towards whom he had no duties. They were defeated and conquered enemies, whom he need not take into account for a moment; their opinions could not affect a noble, and they all owed him respect.

===Chinese===

如果说大自然在体质上把女人造成了一块中性的土地，那么在某些条件下，她完全认同于自己的子女，这并不受禁止。当她不仅给了生命，而且付出了整个生命的时候，你便见到了那件奇妙的、未曾解释也无法解释的事——一个女人对某一个孩子的爱超过对其他所有孩子。这个故事的结局，又为一条已获证明的真理添了一分证明——母亲的位置是无法填补的。母亲预见到危险，比阿尔芒德小姐承认有这种可能要早得多，即使祸事已经铸成。一个防患于未然，一个只能事后补救。况且，处女的母爱里还掺着几分盲目的崇拜，她怎么也狠不下心来责骂一个漂亮男孩。
对世事实际的了解和经办业务的经验，教给了老公证人人一种近乎母亲本能的、带着不信任的敏锐观察力。可是谢内尔在这个家里人微言轻（尤其自从那桩不幸的婚事设想——让一个埃斯格里尼翁家的小姐嫁给一个杜·克鲁瓦西耶——使他失宠之后），他已拿定主意，今后盲目依附家族的信条。他是一名忠于岗位、准备献出生命的普通士兵；哪怕风暴最猛烈的时候，他们也不可能听他的劝告；除非命运像《古董商》里国王的受俸教士那样，把他带到海边——老男爵和他的女儿正被大涨的潮水困住。
杜·克鲁瓦西耶在这孩子所受的畸形教育里瞥见了他的复仇。他希望——借上面那位作者的形象说法——“把羔羊淹死在它母亲的乳汁里”。正是这个希望，造就了他沉默的隐忍，把他脸上引出那种野蛮的微笑。
年轻的维克图尼安伯爵，从能听懂话的时候起，就被教会相信自己的至高无上。王国的一切大贵族都是他的同辈，他唯一的上峰是国王，其余的人类都是他的下级，是与他毫无共同之处、他对之没有任何义务的人。他们是战败的被征服的敌人，他片刻也用不着把他们放在眼里；他们的意见影响不了一位贵族，而他们全都欠他一份敬意。

===Original===

Unluckily, with the rigorous logic of youth, which leads children and young people to proceed to extremes whether good or bad, Victurnien pushed these conclusions to their utmost consequences. His own external advantages, moreover, confirmed him in his beliefs. He had been extraordinarily beautiful as a child; he became as accomplished a young man as any father could wish.
He was of average height, but well proportioned, slender, and almost delicate-looking, but muscular. He had the brilliant blue eyes of the d’Esgrignons, the finely-moulded aquiline nose, the perfect oval of the face, the auburn hair, the white skin, and the graceful gait of his family; he had their delicate extremities, their long taper fingers with the inward curve, and that peculiar distinction of shapeliness of the wrist and instep, that supple felicity of line, which is as sure a sign of race in men as in horses. Adroit and alert in all bodily exercises, and an excellent shot, he handled arms like a St. George, he was a paladin on horseback. In short, he gratified the pride which parents take in their children’s appearance; a pride founded, for that matter, on a just idea of the enormous influence exercised by physical beauty. Personal beauty has this in common with noble birth: it cannot be acquired afterwards; it is everywhere recognized, and often is more valued than either brains or money; beauty has only to appear and triumph; nobody asks more of beauty than that it should simply exist.
Fate had endowed Victurnien, over and above the privileges of good looks and noble birth, with a high spirit, a wonderful aptitude of comprehension, and a good memory. His education, therefore, had been complete. He knew a good deal more than is usually known by young provincial nobles, who develop into highly-distinguished sportsmen, owners of land, and consumers of tobacco; and are apt to treat art, sciences, letters, poetry, or anything offensively above their intellects, cavalierly enough. Such gifts of nature and education surely would one day realize the Marquis d’Esgrignon’s ambitions; he already saw his son a Marshal of France if Victurnien’s tastes were for the army; an ambassador if diplomacy held any attractions for him; a cabinet minister if that career seemed good in his eyes; every place in the state belonged to Victurnien.

===Chinese===

不幸的是，凭着青年人那种不分好坏、一走就走到极端的严苛逻辑，维克图尼安把这些结论推到了终极。况且他外表的优势又印证了他的信念。他小时候出奇地漂亮；长大后，成了任何父亲所能期望的那样完美的青年。
他中等身材，但比例匀称，修长而近乎纤秀，却筋骨结实。他有埃斯格里尼翁家人明亮的蓝眼睛、轮廓优美的鹰钩鼻、完美的鹅蛋脸、栗色的头发、白皙的皮肤和家里特有的优雅步态；他有他们精致的肢体末端，那向内弯曲的修长尖细的手指，手腕和足背那般俊美的独特风韵，那柔韧的线条之美——这在人身上和在马身上一样，是血统的可靠标志。他各种体格运动都敏捷矫健，又是出色的射手，使起武器来像圣乔治，骑起马来是十足的游侠骑士。总之，他满足了父母对子女仪表的骄傲；这种骄傲，说起来，也建立在一个公正的观念上——仪表之美影响巨大。仪表之美与高贵门第有这一点相同：它无法事后获得；它到处受到承认，而且往往比头脑或金钱更受珍视；美只需露面并取胜；谁也不会对美提出别的要求，只要求它存在。
命运除了美貌和高贵门第这两项特权，还赐给维克图尼安高昂的精神、惊人的理解力和良好的记忆力。因此他的教育是完备的。他懂得的，比外省贵族青年通常懂得的多得多——那些青年后来长成了出色的猎手、地主和烟草消耗者，而对艺术、科学、文学、诗歌，或任何冒犯他们智力的东西，动辄以轻慢处之。这样的天赋与教养，想必终有一天能实现德·埃斯格里尼翁侯爵的抱负：倘若维克图尼安爱好军旅，他已看见儿子当上法兰西元帅；倘若外交吸引他，便是大使；倘若那前程在他眼里合适，便是内阁大臣；国家的一切位置都属于维克图尼安。

===Original===

And, most gratifying thought of all for a father, the young Count would have made his way in the world by his own merits even if he had not been a d’Esgrignon.
All through his happy childhood and golden youth, Victurnien had never met with opposition to his wishes. He had been the king of the house; no one curbed the little prince’s will; and naturally he grew up insolent and audacious, selfish as a prince, self-willed as the most high-spirited cardinal of the Middle Ages﻿—defects of character which anyone might guess from his qualities, essentially those of the noble.
The Chevalier was a man of the good old times when the Gray Musketeers were the terror of the Paris theatres, when they horsewhipped the watch and drubbed servers of writs, and played a host of page’s pranks, at which Majesty was wont to smile so long as they were amusing. This charming deceiver and hero of the *ruelles* had no small share in bringing about the disasters which afterwards befell. The amiable old gentleman, with nobody to understand him, was not a little pleased to find a budding Faublas, who looked the part to admiration, and put him in mind of his own young days. So, making no allowance for the difference of the times, he sowed the maxims of a roué of the Encyclopaedic period broadcast in the boy’s mind. He told wicked anecdotes of the reign of His Majesty Louis XV; he glorified the manners and customs of the year 1750; he told of the orgies in *petites maisons*, the follies of courtesans, the capital tricks played on creditors, the manners, in short, which furnished forth Dancourt’s comedies and Beaumarchais’ epigrams. And unfortunately, the corruption lurking beneath the utmost polish tricked itself out in Voltairean wit. If the Chevalier went rather too far at times, he always added as a corrective that a man must always behave himself like a gentleman.
Of all this discourse, Victurnien comprehended just so much as flattered his passions. From the first he saw his old father laughing with the Chevalier. The two elderly men considered that the pride of a d’Esgrignon was a sufficient safeguard against anything unbefitting; as for a dishonorable action, no one in the house imagined that a d’Esgrignon could be guilty of it.

===Chinese===

而对一个父亲来说，最惬意的念头莫过于：这位年轻的伯爵即便不是埃斯格里尼翁家的人，单凭自身的才具也能出人头地。
在幸福的童年和黄金的少年时代，维克图尼安从没遇到过违逆他意愿的事。他一向是家中的国王；谁也不约束这位小王子的意志；于是他自然长成了傲慢而大胆、像王子一样自私、像中世纪最跋扈的红衣主教一样任性的人——这些性格缺陷，谁都可以从他的优点里猜出来；那些优点，正是贵族的优点。
骑士先生是旧时代的人；那时候，灰色火枪手是巴黎剧场的恐怖，他们鞭打巡夜人，痛殴送公文的差役，干出千百件侍童式的恶作剧，只要还有趣，御座上的人总是一笑置之。这位风月场上的迷人骗子和英雄，对后来降临的灾祸负有不小的责任。这位和蔼的老绅士，没有人理解他，如今发现了一个初露头角的小法布拉斯，那模样演这个角色再合适不过，令他想起自己的青年时代，不禁喜出望外。于是，全不考虑时代已经不同，他把百科全书派时代浪荡子的箴言满腔满调地播进男孩的心田。他讲路易十五陛下在位时的邪恶轶事；他颂扬1750年的风俗习尚；他讲小公馆里的纵酒作乐，讲交际花的荒唐，讲对付债主的高明手段，总之讲那些为当库尔的喜剧和博马舍的讽刺诗提供素材的风气。而不幸的是，潜伏在极度优雅之下的腐化，偏偏又用伏尔泰式的机智装点自己。骑士先生即便有时说得过了头，也总要补上一句矫正的话：一个男人行事，永远要像个上等人。
这一套议论，维克图尼安只听懂了那些迎合他情欲的部分。他从小看见老父亲同骑士先生一起说笑。两位老人认为，埃斯格里尼翁家的自尊心足以防范任何不得体的事；至于不名誉的行为，这家里没有一个人想到埃斯格里尼翁家的人会干得出来。

===Original===

**Honor**, the great principle of Monarchy, was planted firm like a beacon in the hearts of the family; it lighted up the least action, it kindled the least thought of a d’Esgrignon. “A d’Esgrignon ought not to permit himself to do such and such a thing; he bears a name which pledges him to make a future worthy of the past”﻿—a noble teaching which should have been sufficient in itself to keep alive the tradition of noblesse﻿—had been, as it were, the burden of Victurnien’s cradle song. He heard them from the old Marquis, from Mlle. Armande, from Chesnel, from the intimates of the house. And so it came to pass that good and evil met, and in equal forces, in the boy’s soul.
At the age of eighteen, Victurnien went into society. He noticed some slight discrepancies between the outer world of the town and the inner world of the Hôtel d’Esgrignon, but he in no wise tried to seek the causes of them. And, indeed, the causes were to be found in Paris. He had yet to learn that the men who spoke their minds out so boldly in evening talk with his father, were extremely careful of what they said in the presence of the hostile persons with whom their interests compelled them to mingle. His own father had won the right of freedom of speech. Nobody dreamed of contradicting an old man of seventy, and besides, everyone was willing to overlook fidelity to the old order of things in a man who had been violently despoiled.
Victurnien was deceived by appearances, and his behavior set up the backs of the townspeople. In his impetuous way he tried to carry matters with too high a hand over some difficulties in the way of sport, which ended in formidable lawsuits, hushed up by Chesnel for money paid down. Nobody dared to tell the Marquis of these things. You may judge of his astonishment if he had heard that his son had been prosecuted for shooting over his lands, his domains, his covers, under the reign of a son of St. Louis! People were too much afraid of the possible consequences to tell him about such trifles, Chesnel said.
The young Count indulged in other escapades in the town.

===Chinese===

荣誉（Honor），君主制的伟大原则，像一座灯塔牢牢立在这家人的心里；它照亮埃斯格里尼翁家最小的举动，点燃他最细微的念头。“埃斯格里尼翁家的人不容许自己做这样那样的事；他背负的这个姓氏，要求他创造无愧于过去的未来”——这崇高的教诲，本该凭自身就足以维系贵族的传统——竟成了维克图尼安摇篮曲的主调。他从小听着老侯爵说，阿尔芒德小姐说，谢内尔说，家里的至交们说。于是，善与恶就在这孩子的灵魂里相遇了，而且势均力敌。
18岁上，维克图尼安进入社交界。他发觉城里的外部世界与埃斯格里尼翁府的内部世界之间有些细小的抵牾，却丝毫不想深究其原因。而原因，其实要到巴黎去找。他还不懂得，那些晚上同他父亲畅所欲言的人，在利益迫使他们与之周旋的敌对者面前，说话是何等小心。他的父亲赢得了言论自由的权利。谁会想去顶撞一个70岁的老人？况且，对一个曾遭暴力剥夺的人，谁都愿意原谅他对旧秩序的忠贞。
维克图尼安被表象迷惑了，他的举止激怒了城里人。他一时冲动，想对打猎遇到的种种纠纷以高压手段了断，结果酿成了几桩吓人的官司，都由谢内尔花钱平息了。谁也不敢把这些事告诉侯爵。倘若侯爵听说，在圣路易之子的治下，他的儿子竟因为在他自己的土地、领地和猎场里打猎而被控告，你可以想见他会多么惊愕！谢内尔说，人们太怕可能的后果，不敢对他讲这些小事。
年轻的伯爵在城里还有别的荒唐事。

===Original===

These the Chevalier regarded as “*amourettes*,” but they cost Chesnel something considerable in portions for forsaken damsels seduced under imprudent promises of marriage: yet other cases there were which came under an article of the Code as to the abduction of minors; and but for Chesnel’s timely intervention, the new law would have been allowed to take its brutal course, and it is hard to say where the Count might have ended. Victurnien grew the bolder for these victories over bourgeois justice. He was so accustomed to be pulled out of scrapes, that he never thought twice before any prank. Courts of law, in his opinion, were bugbears to frighten people who had no hold on him. Things which he would have blamed in common people were for him only pardonable amusements. His disposition to treat the new laws cavalierly while obeying the maxims of a Code for aristocrats, his behavior and character, were all pondered, analyzed, and tested by a few adroit persons in du Croisier’s interests. These folk supported each other in the effort to make the people believe that Liberal slanders were revelations, and that the Ministerial policy at bottom meant a return to the old order of things.
What a bit of luck to find something by way of proof of their assertions! President du Ronceret, and the public prosecutor likewise, lent themselves admirably, so far as was compatible with their duty as magistrates, to the design of letting off the offender as easily as possible; indeed, they went deliberately out of their way to do this, well pleased to raise a Liberal clamor against their overlarge concessions. And so, while seeming to serve the interests of the d’Esgrignons, they stirred up feeling against them. The treacherous de Ronceret had it in his mind to pose as incorruptible at the right moment over some serious charge, with public opinion to back him up. The young Count’s worst tendencies, moreover, were insidiously encouraged by two or three young men who followed in his train, paid court to him, won his favor, and flattered and obeyed him, with a view to confirming his belief in a noble’s supremacy; and all this at a time when a noble’s one chance of preserving his power lay in using it with the utmost discretion for half a century to come.

===Chinese===

骑士先生把这些看作“小小的风流事”，可是为了那些被轻率的结婚许诺诱骗后遭遗弃的姑娘们的赔补，谢内尔破费了不少；还有几桩，几乎落进民法典关于诱拐未成年人的条款；若不是谢内尔及时出面，新法律就要按它严酷的条文办了，伯爵会落到什么下场，殊难预料。这些对资产阶级司法的胜利，使维克图尼安胆子越来越大。他被人从困境里捞出来惯了，做什么荒唐事从来不多想一想。在他看来，法院不过是吓唬那些抓不住他把柄的人的怪物。他若在平民身上见到同样的事定会责难，在自己身上却只是可以原谅的消遣。他这种把新法律不当回事、却谨守贵族法典的做派，他的品行和性格，都被几个奉杜·克鲁瓦西耶之意的机灵人反复揣摩、剖析、检验。这些人互相帮衬，竭力让民众相信：自由党的诽谤实为揭发，而政府的政策骨子里就是要回到旧秩序。
能找到证据坐实他们的说法，多好的运气！法院院长迪·龙瑟雷，还有公诉人，都在不违背法官职责的前提下出色地配合了这套设计，让犯事者尽可能轻易地脱身；他们甚至特意越出常轨去这样做，乐于惹起自由党人埋怨他们让步太大而鼓噪。就这样，他们表面上是为德·埃斯格里尼翁家的利益效力，实际上却在煽起对这家的反感。奸诈的迪·龙瑟雷心中盘算，待到某桩重案发作的有利时机，再摆出铁面无私的姿态，好教舆论给他撑腰。此外，年轻伯爵最坏的倾向，还被两三个跟在他身边的青年暗中怂恿着；这些人逢迎他、讨好他、恭维他、服从他，为的是坚定他对贵族至上的信念；而这个时代，贵族要保住自己的权力，唯一的生机恰恰在于今后半个世纪都以最大的节制去使用它。

===Original===

Du Croisier hoped to reduce the d’Esgrignons to the last extremity of poverty; he hoped to see their castle demolished, and their lands sold piecemeal by auction, through the follies which this harebrained boy was pretty certain to commit. This was as far as he went; he did not think, with President du Ronceret, that Victurnien was likely to give justice another kind of hold upon him. Both men found an ally for their schemes of revenge in Victurnien’s overweening vanity and love of pleasure. President du Ronceret’s son, a lad of seventeen, was admirably fitted for the part of instigator. He was one of the Count’s companions, a new kind of spy in du Croisier’s pay; du Croisier taught him his lesson, set him to track down the noble and beautiful boy through his better qualities, and sardonically prompted him to encourage his victim in his worst faults. Fabien du Ronceret was a sophisticated youth, to whom such a mystification was attractive; he had precisely the keen brain and envious nature which finds in such a pursuit as this the absorbing amusement which a man of an ingenious turn lacks in the provinces.
In three years, between the ages of eighteen and one-and-twenty, Victurnien cost poor Chesnel nearly eighty thousand francs! And this without the knowledge of Mlle. Armande or the Marquis. More than half of the money had been spent in buying off lawsuits; the lad’s extravagance had squandered the rest. Of the Marquis’ income of ten thousand livres, five thousand were necessary for the housekeeping; two thousand more represented Mlle. Armande’s allowance (parsimonious though she was) and the Marquis’ expenses. The handsome young heir-presumptive, therefore, had not a hundred louis to spend. And what sort of figure can a man make on two thousand livres? Victurnien’s tailor’s bills alone absorbed his whole allowance. He had his linen, his clothes, gloves, and perfumery from Paris. He wanted a good English saddle-horse, a tilbury, and a second horse. M. du Croisier had a tilbury and a thoroughbred. Was the bourgeoisie to cut out the noblesse? Then, the young Count must have a man in the d’Esgrignon livery. He prided himself on setting the fashion among young men in the town and the department; he entered that world of luxuries and fancies which suit youth and good looks and wit so well.

===Chinese===
