……必须瞒过，否则除了一死别无选择。他一刻也不耽误，口授了一张收据的格式，让杜·克鲁瓦西耶夫人承认在那张致命汇票出现之前五天，已收讫10万埃居；因为他记得，那阵子杜·克鲁瓦西耶正在乡间督办他妻子田产上的改良工程，不在家。
“现在向我起誓：您会在预审法官面前声明，那笔钱是那个日子收到的。”等杜·克鲁瓦西耶夫人收下钞票、他把收据拿到手里之后，他说。
“那就是撒谎，是不是？”
“小罪。”谢内尔说。
“不先请教我的神师库蒂里耶教士，我做不了这种事。”
“很好，”谢内尔说，“这件事您肯完全听他的指点吗？”
“我答应。”
“还有，见了法官之前，这笔钱不能交给杜·克鲁瓦西耶先生。”
“不交。啊！愿上帝给我力量，让我能站上法庭，在众人面前撑住一个谎言！”
谢内尔吻了吻杜·克鲁瓦西耶夫人的手，随即挺身直立，庄严得像拉斐尔画在梵蒂冈的先知之一。
“您叔父的灵魂正欢喜激动呢，”他说，“您嫁给圣坛与王座之敌这件事，已被永远抹掉了。”——这几句话在杜·克鲁瓦西耶夫人那怯懦的心上留下了强烈的印象。
随后谢内尔猛然想起，还必须把夫人的神师库蒂里耶教士这一关拿下。他知道，虔诚的灵魂一旦为本方出战，能何等执拗地为自家主张的胜利奔走，所以他想尽早把教会的支持抓到手。于是他到埃斯格里尼翁府，唤醒阿尔芒德小姐，向她报告了当夜的经过，打发她去把主教大人本人请上火线。
“啊，上天的主！您必得拯救埃斯格里尼翁家！”他慢慢往家走，一面感叹，“这桩事如今要发展成一场法庭上的战斗了。我们对面的这些人，各有各的欲望和利益；这反倒好办，什么都套得出来。这个杜·克鲁瓦西耶钻了公诉人不在的空子；公诉人忠于我们，可议会一开会他就去了巴黎。那么，他们使了什么手段，把他的副手拉拢过去的呢？

===Original===

They have induced him to take up the charge without consulting his chief. This mystery must be looked into, and the ground surveyed tomorrow; and then, perhaps, when I have unraveled this web of theirs, I will go back to Paris to set great powers at work through Mme. de Maufrigneuse.”
So he reasoned, poor, aged, clear-sighted wrestler, before he lay down half dead with bearing the weight of so much emotion and fatigue. And yet, before he fell asleep he ran a searching eye over the list of magistrates, taking all their secret ambitions into account, casting about for ways of influencing them, calculating his chances in the coming struggle. Chesnel’s prolonged scrutiny of consciences, given in a condensed form, will perhaps serve as a picture of the judicial world in a country town.
Magistrates and officials generally are obliged to begin their career in the provinces; judicial ambition there ferments. At the outset every man looks towards Paris; they all aspire to shine in the vast theatre where great political causes come before the courts, and the higher branches of the legal profession are closely connected with the palpitating interests of society. But few are called to that paradise of the man of law, and nine-tenths of the profession are bound sooner or later to regard themselves as shelved for good in the provinces. Wherefore, every Tribunal of First Instance and every Court-Royal is sharply divided in two. The first section has given up hope, and is either torpid or content; content with the excessive respect paid to office in a country town, or torpid with tranquillity. The second section is made up of the younger sort, in whom the desire of success is untempered as yet by disappointment, and of the really clever men urged on continually by ambition as with a goad; and these two are possessed with a sort of fanatical belief in their order.
At this time the younger men were full of Royalist zeal against the enemies of the Bourbons. The most insignificant deputy official was dreaming of conducting a prosecution, and praying with all his might for one of those political cases which bring a man’s zeal into prominence, draw the attention of the higher powers, and mean advancement for King’s men. Was there a member of an official staff of prosecuting counsel who could hear of a Bonapartist conspiracy breaking out somewhere else without a feeling of envy?

===Chinese===

“他们诱使他没请示上司就把案子接了过去。这个谜必须查个明白，明天先勘察一番地势；然后，也许等我理清了他们这张网，再回巴黎，借德·莫弗里涅斯夫人发动大人物们行动起来。”
这位可怜的、年迈的、目光清明的角斗士，就这样思前想后，直到心力交瘁、半死不活地躺下。然而入睡之前，他还在用一双锐利的眼睛把法官们的名单扫了一遍，掂量着他们各自的隐秘野心，琢磨着各个击破的门路，计算着这场即将到来的搏斗的胜算。谢内尔这场漫长的良心检阅，压缩成一篇，或许正好当作外省司法界的一幅图画。
法官和官员一般都得从外省起步；司法界的野心便在那里发酵。起初人人都望着巴黎；人人渴望在那个大舞台上露脸——重大政治案件在那里开庭，法律职业的上层与社会的敏感利益紧紧相连。可是蒙召进入这法律人天堂的寥寥无几，这一行十分之九的人迟早认定自己已被永久搁置在外省。因此，每一所初级法院、每一所王家法院都截然分成两派。第一派已经断了念头，或麻木，或知足：知足于外省小城对官职的过分敬重，或麻木于四境的太平。第二派由两类人组成：一是年轻人，成功的欲望尚未被失意消磨；一是真正聪明的人，被野心像牛虻一样不断驱赶着；这两类人都对自己的阶层抱着一种狂热的迷信。
这个时候，年轻一辈满腔保王的热情，仇视波旁的敌人。最不起眼的副职官员也梦想着主持一场公诉，拼命祈求摊上一桩政治案件——那种案子能显出一个人的热忱，引起上头的注意，意味着国王的人加官进爵。公诉处的属员里，有谁听说别处冒出一起波拿巴分子的阴谋而不眼红吗？

===Original===

Where was the man that did not burn to discover a Caron, or a Berton, or a revolt of some sort? With reasons of State, and the necessity of diffusing the monarchical spirit throughout France as their basis, and a fierce ambition stirred up whenever party spirit ran high, these ardent politicians on their promotion were lucid, clear-sighted, and perspicacious. They kept up a vigorous detective system throughout the kingdom; they did the work of spies, and urged the nation along a path of obedience, from which it had no business to swerve.
Justice, thus informed with monarchical enthusiasm, atoned for the errors of the ancient parliaments, and walked, perhaps, too ostentatiously hand in hand with religion. There was more zeal than discretion shown; but justice sinned not so much in the direction of machiavelism as by giving the candid expression to its views, when those views appeared to be opposed to the general interests of a country which must be put safely out of reach of revolutions. But taken as a whole, there was still too much of the bourgeois element in the administration; it was too readily moved by petty liberal agitation; and as a result, it was inevitable that it should incline sooner or later to the Constitutional party, and join ranks with the bourgeoisie in the day of battle. In the great body of legal functionaries, as in other departments of the administration, there was not wanting a certain hypocrisy, or rather that spirit of imitation which always leads France to model herself on the Court, and, quite unintentionally, to deceive the powers that be.
Officials of both complexions were to be found in the court in which young d’Esgrignon’s fate depended. M. le Président du Ronceret and an elderly judge, Blondet by name, represented the section of functionaries shelved for good, and resigned to stay where they were; while the young and ambitious party comprised the examining magistrate M. Camusot, and his deputy M. Michu, appointed through the interests of the Cinq-Cygnes, and certain of promotion to the Court of Appeal of Paris at the first opportunity.
President du Ronceret held a permanent post; it was impossible to turn him out.

===Chinese===

谁不烧心烧肺地想揪出一个卡龙、一个贝尔东，或某一场暴动？这些一门心思往上爬的热血政客，以国家理由和向全法兰西灌输君主精神之必要为基础，党争一起野心便熊熊鼓噪；他们目光清明，洞若观火，明察秋毫。他们在全国维持着一套高效的侦缉体系；他们干着密探的营生，催赶国民沿着服从的道路前进——国民也本无权偏离这条道路。
司法界这样浸透了君主主义的热情，替旧日高等法院的过失赎了罪，也许还过于张扬地同宗教携手而行。表现出来的热忱多于审慎；但司法的罪过倒不在马基雅维利主义那个方向，而在于它过于坦率地表白自己的观点——当那些观点看来有悖于国家的总体利益时；而这个国家，必须被安置到革命够不着的安全地带。但总体而论，行政机构里的资产阶级成分仍然太重；小小的自由派鼓噪就容易把它撼动；结果是，它迟早倒向宪政党，在大战之日同资产阶级合流，势所必然。在司法官吏的庞大队伍里，同行政其他部门一样，并不缺少某种伪善，或者说得确切些，那种模仿精神——它总引着法兰西以宫廷为样板，并且在全然无意之中欺骗着当权的诸公。
两色人等，在决定小德·埃斯格里尼翁命运的这家法院里都找得到。院长迪·龙瑟雷先生和一位名叫布隆代的老法官，代表那派被永久搁置、认命留守的官员；而年轻野心的一派，则包括预审法官卡缪索先生和他的副手米许先生——后者靠桑-赛涅家的势力谋得职位，一心指望头一个机会就升到巴黎上诉法院。
院长迪·龙瑟雷占的是终身职位；谁也撵不走他。

===Original===

The aristocratic party declined to give him what he considered to be his due, socially speaking; so he declared for the bourgeoisie, glossed over his disappointment with the name of independence, and failed to realize that his opinions condemned him to remain a president of a court of the first instance for the rest of his life. Once started in this track the sequence of events led du Ronceret to place his hopes of advancement on the triumph of du Croisier and the Left. He was in no better odor at the Prefecture than at the Court-Royal. He was compelled to keep on good terms with the authorities; the Liberals distrusted him, consequently he belonged to neither party. He was obliged to resign his chances of election to du Croisier, he exercised no influence, and played a secondary part. The false position reacted on his character; he was soured and discontented; he was tired of political ambiguity, and privately had made up his mind to come forward openly as leader of the Liberal party, and so to strike ahead of du Croisier. His behavior in the d’Esgrignon affair was the first step in this direction. To begin with, he was an admirable representative of that section of the middle classes which allows its petty passions to obscure the wider interests of the country; a class of crotchety politicians, upholding the government one day and opposing it the next, compromising every cause and helping none; helpless after they have done the mischief till they set about brewing more; unwilling to face their own incompetence, thwarting authority while professing to serve it. With a compound of arrogance and humility they demand of the people more submission than kings expect, and fret their souls because those above them are not brought down to their level, as if greatness could be little, as if power existed without force.
President du Ronceret was a tall, spare man with a receding forehead and scanty, auburn hair. He was walleyed, his complexion was blotched, his lips thin and hard, his scarcely audible voice came out like the husky wheezings of asthma. He had for a wife a great, solemn, clumsy creature, tricked out in the most ridiculous fashion, and outrageously overdressed. Mme.

===Chinese===

贵族党不肯给他他自认为应得的社交地位；于是他声明站在资产阶级一边，拿“独立”二字给自己的失意涂脂抹粉，却看不出他的主张恰恰判处他终身当一个初级法院院长。既然走上了这条道，事态的发展便引导迪·龙瑟雷把升迁的希望押在杜·克鲁瓦西耶和左派的胜利上。他在省长署的名声并不比在王家法院强。他不得不与当局保持和睦；自由党人又不信任他；结果两党都容不下他。他只好把竞选的机会让给杜·克鲁瓦西耶，发挥不了影响，扮演着二流角色。这虚假的处境反过来作用于他的性格；他变得乖戾不满；他厌倦了政治上的暖昧，私下打定主意，要公开站出来做自由党的领袖，抢到杜·克鲁瓦西耶前头去。他在埃斯格里尼翁一案中的行径，就是朝这个方向迈出的第一步。首先，他是中产阶级中那个阶层的绝妙代表——这阶层让鸡毛蒜皮的私怨遮蔽了国家更广的利益；一帮古怪的政客，今天拥护政府，明天反对政府，败坏每一桩事业而一无所助；闯了祸便束手无策，直到着手酿成新的祸；不肯正视自己的无能，口称效力当局，实则处处掣肘。他们以傲慢与卑屈的化合物，向人民索要比国王还多的顺从，又因为上面的人不肯降到他们的水平而气结愁苦——仿佛伟大可以渺小，仿佛权力可以不凭实力而存在。
院长迪·龙瑟雷是个瘦高个儿，额角后削，头顶稀疏的栗色头发。他双眼斜视，脸上斑块点点，嘴唇薄而硬，说话声音低得几乎听不见，出来时活像哮喘病人沙哑的喘息。他娶的太太是个高大、板正、笨手笨脚的活物，打扮得再可笑没有了，衣衫俗艳得吓人。院长夫人

===Original===

la Présidente gave herself the airs of a queen; she wore vivid colors, and always appeared at balls adorned with the turban, dear to the British female, and lovingly cultivated in out-of-the-way districts in France. Each of the pair had an income of four or five thousand francs, which with the President’s salary, reached a total of some twelve thousand. In spite of a decided tendency to parsimony, vanity required that they should receive one evening in the week. Du Croisier might import modern luxury into the town, M. and Mme. de Ronceret were faithful to the old traditions. They had always lived in the old-fashioned house belonging to Mme. du Ronceret, and had made no changes in it since their marriage. The house stood between a garden and a courtyard. The gray old gable end, with one window in each story, gave upon the road. High walls enclosed the garden and the yard, but the space taken up beneath them in the garden by a walk shaded with chestnut trees was filled in the yard by a row of outbuildings. An old rust-devoured iron gate in the garden wall balanced the yard gateway, a huge, double-leaved carriage entrance with a buttress on either side, and a mighty shell on the top. The same shell was repeated over the house-door.
The whole place was gloomy, close, and airless. The row of iron-gated openings in the opposite wall, as you entered, reminded you of prison windows. Every passerby could look in through the railings to see how the garden grew; the flowers in the little square borders never seemed to thrive there.
The drawing-room on the ground floor was lighted by a single window on the side of the street, and a French window above a flight of steps, which gave upon the garden. The dining-room on the other side of the great antechamber, with its windows also looking out into the garden, was exactly the same size as the drawing-room, and all three apartments were in harmony with the general air of gloom. It wearied your eyes to look at the ceilings all divided up by huge painted crossbeams and adorned with a feeble lozenge pattern or a rosette in the middle. The paint was old, startling in tint, and begrimed with smoke. The sun had faded the heavy silk curtains in the drawing-room; the old-fashioned Beauvais tapestry which covered the white-painted furniture had lost all its color with wear.

===Chinese===

……摆出一副王后的架子；她爱穿艳色，出席舞会总裹着那条深受英国女人喜爱、又被法国偏僻地区精心培育的头巾式头饰。夫妇俩各有四五千法郎的年金，加上院长的薪俸，总数约1.2万。尽管生性相当吝啬，虚荣心却要求他们每星期接待一晚宾客。杜·克鲁瓦西耶尽可以把现代奢华搬进城来，迪·龙瑟雷先生和夫人却忠于老传统。他们一直住在杜·龙瑟雷夫人的那所老式宅子里，婚后从未改动分毫。宅子夹在花园与院子之间。灰色的老山墙一面临街，每层开着一扇窗。高墙围着花园和院子；墙下，花园里是一条栗树成荫的步道，院子里则是一溜附属用房。花园墙上一扇锈蚀的老铁门，与院子的大门遥遥相对——那是两扇的通车大门，两侧各有一道扶壁，顶上安着一只巨大的贝壳。同样的贝壳又在正门上方重复了一遍。
整个宅子阴沉、闭塞、不通风。一进门，对面墙上一排装铁栅的开口，叫人想起监狱的窗户。过路人都能隔着栏杆看清花园长得如何；方形小花畦里的花，在那儿似乎从来长不好。
底层的客厅只有临街一扇窗采光，另有一扇落地窗开在台阶之上，通向花园。大前厅另一侧的餐厅，窗户也朝花园开，大小与客厅分毫不差；三间屋子与全宅的阴郁气氛正相协调。望一眼天花板就叫人眼睛发累：巨大的彩绘横梁把顶面分割成块，中间缀一个没精打采的菱形图案或一朵玫瑰花饰。漆色陈旧，色调骇人，烟垢斑驳。客厅里厚重的丝帘已被太阳晒褪了色；罩在白漆家具上的博韦旧壁毯，也磨得颜色尽失。

===Original===

A Louis Quinze clock on the chimneypiece stood between two extravagant, branched sconces filled with yellow wax candles, which the Présidente only lighted on occasions when the old-fashioned rock-crystal chandelier emerged from its green wrapper. Three card-tables, covered with threadbare baize, and a backgammon box, sufficed for the recreations of the company; and Mme. du Ronceret treated them to such refreshments as cider, chestnuts, pastry puffs, glasses of eau sucrée, and homemade orgeat. For some time past she had made a practice of giving a party once a fortnight, when tea and some pitiable attempts at pastry appeared to grace the occasion.
Once a quarter the du Roncerets gave a grand three-course dinner, which made a great sensation in the town, a dinner served up in execrable ware, but prepared with the science for which the provincial cook is remarkable. It was a Gargantuan repast, which lasted for six whole hours, and by abundance the President tried to vie with du Croisier’s elegance.
And so du Ronceret’s life and its accessories were just what might have been expected from his character and his false position. He felt dissatisfied at home without precisely knowing what was the matter; but he dared not go to any expense to change existing conditions, and was only too glad to put by seven or eight thousand francs every year, so as to leave his son Fabien a handsome private fortune. Fabien du Ronceret had no mind for the magistracy, the bar, or the civil service, and his pronounced turn for doing nothing drove his parent to despair.
On this head there was rivalry between the President and the Vice-President, old M. Blondet. M. Blondet, for a long time past, had been sedulously cultivating an acquaintance between his son and the Blandureau family. The Blandureaus were well-to-do linen manufacturers, with an only daughter, and it was on this daughter that the President had fixed his choice of a wife for Fabien. Now, Joseph Blondet’s marriage with Mlle. Blandureau depended on his nomination to the post which his father, old Blondet, hoped to obtain for him when he himself should retire. But President du Ronceret, in underhand ways, was thwarting the old man’s plans, and working indirectly upon the Blandureaus.

===Chinese===

壁炉台上一座路易十五式座钟，立在两盏浮夸的多枝烛台之间，插着黄蜡烛；只有那盏老式水晶枝形吊灯从绿布罩里请出来的时候，院长夫人才点它们。三张蒙着磨秃台呢的牌桌，一只十五子棋盒，就够宾客们消遣了；杜·龙瑟雷夫人款待大家的是苹果酒、栗子、奶油泡芙、一杯杯糖水，和自制的杏仁糖浆。近来她养成了习惯，每两周开一次晚会，届时便有茶水和几样可怜巴巴的糕点出来装点场面。
每季度一次，迪·龙瑟雷夫妇设一顿丰盛的正餐，全城为之轰动；餐具恶劣得可怕，烹调却拿出了外省厨子赖以成名的那门科学。那是一席巨人宴，从头到尾吃足六个钟头；院长想靠数量上的丰盛，同比一比杜·克鲁瓦西耶的雅致。
于是，迪·龙瑟雷的生活及全部附属品，恰如人们从他的性格和虚假处境所料想的那样。他在家里浑身不自在，又说不清毛病出在哪；可他不敢破费分文去改变现状，巴不得每年攒下七八千法郎，好给儿子法比安留下一份可观的私产。法比安·迪·龙瑟雷对法官、律师或行政都没有兴趣，他那出了名的好吃懒做的脾性，把老子气得绝望。
恰恰在这件事上，院长同副院长老布隆代先生较上了劲。很久以来，布隆代先生一直在精心撮合他的儿子同布朗迪罗家的来往。布朗迪罗家是殷实的亚麻布商，只有一个独生女儿；而院长给法比安相中的媳妇，正是这个女儿。然而约瑟夫·布隆代同布朗迪罗小姐的婚事，系于老布隆代指望自己告老时为儿子谋得的那个职位。可院长迪·龙瑟雷正在暗中使绊子，破坏老人的计划，又间接地做布朗迪罗家的工作。

===Original===

Indeed, if it had not been for this affair of young d’Esgrignon’s, the astute President might have cut them out, father and son, for their rivals were very much richer.
M. Blondet, the victim of the machiavelian President’s intrigues, was one of the curious figures which lie buried away in the provinces like old coins in a crypt. He was at that time a man of sixty-seven or thereabouts, but he carried his years well; he was very tall, and in build reminded you of the canons of the good old times. The smallpox had riddled his face with numberless dints, and spoilt the shape of his nose by imparting to it a gimlet-like twist; it was a countenance by no means lacking in character, very evenly tinted with a diffused red, lighted up by a pair of bright little eyes, with a sardonic look in them, while a certain sarcastic twitch of the purpled lips gave expression to that feature.
Before the Revolution broke out, Blondet senior had been a barrister; afterwards he became the public accuser, and one of the mildest of those formidable functionaries. Goodman Blondet, as they used to call him, deadened the force of the new doctrines by acquiescing in them all, and putting none of them in practice. He had been obliged to send one or two nobles to prison; but his further proceedings were marked with such deliberation, that he brought them through to the 9th Thermidor with a dexterity which won respect for him on all sides. As a matter of fact, Goodman Blondet ought to have been President of the Tribunal, but when the courts of law were reorganized he had been set aside; Napoleon’s aversion for Republicans was apt to reappear in the smallest appointments under his government. The qualification of ex-public accuser, written in the margin of the list against Blondet’s name, set the Emperor inquiring of Cambacérès whether there might not be some scion of an ancient parliamentary stock to appoint instead. The consequence was that du Ronceret, whose father had been a councillor of parliament, was nominated to the presidency; but, the Emperor’s repugnance notwithstanding, Cambacérès allowed Blondet to remain on the bench, saying that the old barrister was one of the best jurisconsults in France.

===Chinese===

说真的，若不是出了小德·埃斯格里尼翁这桩案子，精明的院长说不定真能把这一对父子挤掉，因为他们的对手阔得多。
布隆代先生，这位马基雅维利式院长的阴谋的牺牲品，是那些埋在外省的奇特人物之一，像地窖里的古钱。他那时67岁上下，可是显老得不多；他个子很高，身架叫人想起旧日的好时光里的那些座堂神父。天花在他脸上麻出无数坑，又把他的鼻子拧成螺旋锥的形状，毁了它的端正；这副面孔绝不缺少性格，满面匀净的漫散的红，一双明亮的小眼睛点亮它，眼里带着讥诮，紫红的嘴唇一扯讪笑，又给这副眉目添了神采。
革命爆发之前，老布隆代当过律师；后来他成了公诉人，而且是那些可怕的官吏中最温和的一个。好老头布隆代——人们从前就这么叫他——对新学说一律点头，一条也不实行，以此磨钝了它们的锋芒。他不得已把一两位贵族送进了监狱；但他此后办案的迟缓绵密滴水不漏，硬是把他们平安拖到热月九日，那份手腕为他赢得了四面八方的敬重。事实上，好老头布隆代本该当上法院院长，可是司法机关重组时他被撇到了一边；拿破仑对共和党人的厌恶，在他治下最微小的任命里也总要冒头。“前公诉人”几个字写在名单页边、布隆代名字的对面，惹得皇帝问康巴塞雷斯：有没有什么旧高等法院世家的子弟可以委派。结果是父亲当过高等法院参事的迪·龙瑟雷被提名为院长；不过，尽管皇帝反感，康巴塞雷斯还是让布隆代留在了审判席上，说这位老律师是法兰西最好的法学家之一。

===Original===

Blondet’s talents, his knowledge of the old law of the land and subsequent legislation, should by rights have brought him far in his profession; but he had this much in common with some few great spirits: he entertained a prodigious contempt for his own special knowledge, and reserved all his pretentions, leisure, and capacity for a second pursuit unconnected with the law. To this pursuit he gave his almost exclusive attention. The good man was passionately fond of gardening. He was in correspondence with some of the most celebrated amateurs; it was his ambition to create new species; he took an interest in botanical discoveries, and lived, in short, in the world of flowers. Like all florists, he had a predilection for one particular plant; the pelargonium was his especial favorite. The court, the cases that came before it, and his outward life were as nothing to him compared with the inward life of fancies and abundant emotions which the old man led. He fell more and more in love with his flower-seraglio; and the pains which he bestowed on his garden, the sweet round of the labors of the months, held Goodman Blondet fast in his greenhouse. But for that hobby he would have been a deputy under the Empire, and shone conspicuous beyond a doubt in the Corps Legislatif.
His marriage was the second cause of his obscurity. As a man of forty, he was rash enough to marry a girl of eighteen, by whom he had a son named Joseph in the first year of their marriage. Three years afterwards Mme. Blondet, then the prettiest woman in the town, inspired in the prefect of the department a passion which ended only with her death. The prefect was the father of her second son Émile; the whole town knew this, old Blondet himself knew it. The wife who might have roused her husband’s ambition, who might have won him away from his flowers, positively encouraged the judge in his botanical tastes. She no more cared to leave the place than the prefect cared to leave his prefecture so long as his mistress lived.
Blondet felt himself unequal at his age to a contest with a young wife. He sought consolation in his greenhouse, and engaged a very pretty servant-maid to assist him to tend his ever-changing bevy of beauties. So while the judge potted, pricked out, watered, layered, slipped, blended, and induced his flowers to break, Mme.

===Chinese===

布隆代的才干，他对本土旧法和此后种种立法的通晓，照理早该把他带到本行的高处；可是他同少数几个大心灵有一点相通：他对自己那一门专门学问怀着惊人的轻蔑，把全部抱负、闲暇和才力，都留给了一门同法律不相干的第二事业，对它倾注了几乎是全部的心神。这位好人酷爱园艺。他同几位最著名的爱好者通信；他的雄心是培育新种；他关心植物学的新发现；总之，他生活在花的世界里。同所有养花人一样，他偏爱某一种花；天竺葵是他的心头好。法院、审理的案件、他的外在生活，同这位老人内心那充满幻想与丰盛情感的生活相比，在他眼里轻如鸿毛。他越来越爱他的花儿后宫；他为花园下的功夫，各月农事的甜蜜轮回，把好老头布隆代牢牢拴在了温室里。若没有这个癖好，他在帝国时期早当上议员，而且毫无疑问在立法团里大放异彩。
他的婚姻是他默默无闻的第二个原因。四十岁上，他冒失地娶了一个18岁的姑娘，婚后头一年生了个儿子，取名约瑟夫。三年之后，布隆代夫人——当时全城最美的女人——激起本省省长的一段情，一直延续到她去世。省长是她第二个儿子埃米尔的父亲；全城都知道，老布隆代自己心里也清楚。这位太太本可以唤醒丈夫的野心，把他从花丛里拉出来，她却反倒怂恿法官发展他的植物学趣味。她不肯离开此地，正如省长在情妇在世之日不肯离开他的省长任所。
布隆代觉得，到了这个年纪，同年轻的妻子较量力不从心。他便到温室里寻求安慰，雇了一个十分漂亮的女仆，帮他照料那群不断更新的花中佳丽。于是，法官上盆、移栽、浇水、压条、扦插、杂交，诱导他的花木变种；而布隆代夫人

===Original===

Blondet spent his substance on the dress and finery in which she shone at the prefecture. One interest alone had power to draw her away from the tender care of a romantic affection which the town came to admire in the end; and this interest was Émile’s education. The child of love was a bright and pretty boy, while Joseph was no less heavy and plain-featured. The old judge, blinded by paternal affection, loved Joseph as his wife loved Émile.
For a dozen years M. Blondet bore his lot with perfect resignation. He shut his eyes to his wife’s intrigue with a dignified, well-bred composure, quite in the style of an eighteenth century *grand seigneur*; but, like all men with a taste for a quiet life, he could cherish a profound dislike, and he hated his younger son. When his wife died, therefore, in 1818, he turned the intruder out of the house, and packed him off to Paris to study law on an allowance of twelve hundred francs for all resource, nor could any cry of distress extract another penny from his purse. Émile Blondet would have gone under if it had not been for his real father.
M. Blondet’s house was one of the prettiest in the town. It stood almost opposite the prefecture, with a neat little court in front. A row of old-fashioned iron railings between two brickwork piers enclosed it from the street; and a low wall, also of brick, with a second row of railings along the top, connected the piers with the neighboring house. The little court, a space about ten fathoms in width by twenty in length, was cut in two by a brick pathway which ran from the gate to the house door between a border on either side. Those borders were always renewed; at every season of the year they exhibited a successful show of blossom, to the admiration of the public. All along the back of the gardenbeds a quantity of climbing plants grew up and covered the walls of the neighboring houses with a magnificent mantle; the brickwork piers were hidden in clusters of honeysuckle; and, to crown all, in a couple of terra-cotta vases at the summit, a pair of acclimatized cactuses displayed to the astonished eyes of the ignorant those thick leaves bristling with spiny defences which seem to be due to some plant disease.
It was a plain-looking house, built of brick, with brickwork arches above the windows, and bright green Venetian shutters to make it gay.

===Chinese===

……则把家财花在衣饰和行头上，好让她在省长官邸光彩照人。只有一件事能把她从那段柔情蜜意的体贴中拉开——那段情缘，全城后来都艳羡不已；这件事就是埃米尔的教育。爱情的结晶是个聪明俊俏的孩子，而约瑟夫却同样的迟钝而相貌平庸。老法官被父爱蒙住了眼，爱约瑟夫，一如妻子爱埃米尔。
十几年来，布隆代先生怀着十足的听天由命承受着他的命运。他对妻子的私情闭目不视，一派尊严而又有教养的镇定，很有些十八世纪大贵族的气度；可是，同一切嗜好清静的人一样，他能怀有深藏的嫌恶；他恨他的小儿子。因此，1818年妻子去世后，他把那个闯入者逐出了家门，打发他去巴黎学法律，全部生活费一年1200法郎；任凭怎样哭穷叫苦，也休想从他钱袋里多抠出一个铜子。若不是他的生身父亲，埃米尔·布隆代早就沉下去了。
布隆代先生的宅子是全城最漂亮的宅子之一，几乎正对着省长官署，门前一方整洁的小院。两座砖墩之间一排老式铁栏杆把它同大街隔开；一道同样是砖砌的矮墙，墙顶又装一排栏杆，把砖墩同邻宅连接起来。小院长约十突阿斯宽、二十突阿斯长，一条砖道从院门直通正房，两侧各带一线花畦。花畦常换常新；一年四季都开出成功的花展，令公众赞叹。花畦背后爬满了藤蔓植物，给邻家的墙披上一件华丽的斗篷；砖墩藏在一簇簇忍冬里；最妙的是，墩顶两只陶盆里，一对驯化了的仙人掌，向无知者惊愕的眼睛展示着它们那肥厚多刺、仿佛害了什么植物病的叶子。
房子本身样子朴素，砖砌的，窗上砌着砖拱，装着鲜绿色的百叶窗，添几分明快。

===Original===

Through the glass door you could look straight across the house to the opposite glass door, at the end of a long passage, and down the central alley in the garden beyond; while through the windows of the dining-room and drawing-room, which extended, like the passage, from back to front of the house, you could often catch further glimpses of the flowerbeds in a garden of about two acres in extent. Seen from the road, the brickwork harmonized with the fresh flowers and shrubs, for two centuries had overlaid it with mosses and green and russet tints. No one could pass through the town without falling in love with a house with such charming surroundings, so covered with flowers and mosses to the roof-ridge, where two pigeons of glazed crockery ware were perched by way of ornament.
M. Blondet possessed an income of about four thousand livres derived from land, besides the old house in the town. He meant to avenge his wrongs legitimately enough. He would leave his house, his lands, his seat on the bench to his son Joseph, and the whole town knew what he meant to do. He had made a will in that son’s favor; he had gone as far as the Code will permit a man to go in the way of disinheriting one child to benefit another; and what was more, he had been putting by money for the past fifteen years to enable his lout of a son to buy back from Émile that portion of his father’s estate which could not legally be taken away from him.
Émile Blondet thus turned adrift had contrived to gain distinction in Paris, but so far it was rather a name than a practical result. Émile’s indolence, recklessness, and happy-go-lucky ways drove his real father to despair; and when that father died, a half-ruined man, turned out of office by one of the political reactions so frequent under the Restoration, it was with a mind uneasy as to the future of a man endowed with the most brilliant qualities.
Émile Blondet found support in a friendship with a Mlle. de Troisville, whom he had known before her marriage with the Comte de Montcornet. His mother was living when the Troisvilles came back after the emigration; she was related to the family, distantly it is true, but the connection was close enough to allow her to introduce Émile to the house. She, poor woman, foresaw the future.

===Chinese===

透过玻璃门，可以一直望穿整座房子，望见长廊尽头对面的那扇玻璃门，再望进园中的中央小径；而餐厅和客厅的窗——它们同长廊一样，从屋后直通屋前——又常常让你再瞥见几眼花坛，那座园子约有阿尔邦两亩地。从大路望去，砖色同鲜花灌木相映成趣，因为两个世纪的风霜已给砖面覆上了苔藓和青褐的色调。路过这座城的人，没有不爱上这所宅子的——环境这样迷人，花朵和苔藓一直爬满屋脊，脊上还蹲着两只釉陶的鸽子作装饰。
布隆代先生除城里这所老宅外，还有约4000里弗尔的地租收入。他打算用完全合法的方式为自己所受的委屈复仇。他要把这宅子、这些地、这个法官席位都留给儿子约瑟夫，全城也都知道他的打算。他已立下有利于这个儿子的遗嘱；民法典容许怎样剥夺一个孩子去贴补另一个孩子，他就做到了极限；更有甚者，十五年来他一直在攒钱，好让他那憨儿子将来从埃米尔手里，把法律不容剥夺的那份父亲遗产买回来。
就这样被扫地出门的埃米尔·布隆代，在巴黎闯出了名头，不过到目前为止，名声大于实惠。埃米尔的懒散、挥霍和得过且过，使他的生身父亲绝望；当那位父亲去世时——他已败了一半家，被复辟时期屡见不鲜的一次政治回潮赶下了台——心里还在为这个天赋卓越的人的前途发愁。
埃米尔·布隆代靠同一位德·特鲁瓦维尔小姐的友谊撑持着；早在她嫁给蒙科内伯爵之前，他就认识她。特鲁瓦维尔家流亡归来时，他母亲还在世；她同这家沾点亲，确切说是远亲，但亲得足够让她把埃米尔引见进那座宅门。她，可怜的女人，预见了将来。

===Original===

She knew that when she died her son would lose both mother and father, a thought which made death doubly bitter, so she tried to interest others in him. She encouraged the liking that sprang up between Émile and the eldest daughter of the house of Troisville; but while the liking was exceedingly strong on the young lady’s part, a marriage was out of the question. It was a romance on the pattern of *Paul et Virginie*. Mme. Blondet did what she could to teach her son to look to the Troisvilles, to found a lasting attachment on a children’s game of “make-believe” love, which was bound to end as boy-and-girl romances usually do. When Mlle. de Troisville’s marriage with General Montcornet was announced, Mme. Blondet, a dying woman, went to the bride and solemnly implored her never to abandon Émile, and to use her influence for him in society in Paris, whither the General’s fortune summoned her to shine.
Luckily for Émile, he was able to make his own way. He made his appearance, at the age of twenty, as one of the masters of modern literature; and met with no less success in the society into which he was launched by the father who at first could afford to bear the expense of the young man’s extravagance. Perhaps Émile’s precocious celebrity and the good figure that he made strengthened the bonds of his friendship with the Countess. Perhaps Mme. de Montcornet, with the Russian blood in her veins (her mother was the daughter of the Princess Scherbelloff), might have cast off the friend of her childhood if he had been a poor man struggling with all his might among the difficulties which beset a man of letters in Paris; but by the time that the real strain of Émile’s adventurous life began, their attachment was unalterable on either side. He was looked upon as one of the leading lights of journalism when young d’Esgrignon met him at his first supper party in Paris; his acknowledged position in the world of letters was very high, and he towered above his reputation. Goodman Blondet had not the faintest conception of the power which the Constitutional Government had given to the press; nobody ventured to talk in his presence of the son of whom he refused to hear. And so it came to pass that he knew nothing of Émile whom he had cursed and Émile’s greatness.

===Chinese===

她知道自己一死，儿子就同时失去母亲和父亲；这个念头使死亡加倍苦涩，所以她设法让别人也来关心他。她鼓励埃米尔同特鲁瓦维尔家大小姐之间生出的情愫；只是小姐那边的情意固然极深，婚姻却无从谈起。那是一段《保罗与薇吉妮》式的罗曼史。布隆代夫人竭尽所能，教儿子依恃特鲁瓦维尔家，在一场孩童“过家家”式的爱情上建立持久的情谊——这种情谊，注定要像少男少女的罗曼史那样收场。德·特鲁瓦维尔小姐同蒙科内将军的婚事宣布时，布隆代夫人已是垂死之人，她去找新娘，郑重恳求她永不抛弃埃米尔，要在巴黎社交界为他施加影响——将军的家财正召唤她去那里大放光华。
埃米尔幸而能够自闯前程。20岁上，他便以当代文学的一位大师的身份登场；在社交界也同样成功——把他引进那个圈子的是他的生父，起初他还出得起钱，供得起这青年的挥霍。也许埃米尔早慧的名声和他露出的漂亮身手，加固了他同伯爵夫人的友谊。德·蒙科内夫人血管里流着俄国的血（她母亲是谢尔贝洛娃公主的女儿），倘若埃米尔只是个在巴黎文人的重重困厄中拼命挣扎的穷汉，她说不定早把这个童年的朋友甩了；可是等到埃米尔冒险生涯真正的重负开始时，两边的情谊都已牢不可破。小德·埃斯格里尼翁在巴黎的第一顿宵夜里遇见他时，他已被视为新闻界的一盏明灯；他在文学界的公认地位极高，本人的分量还在名声之上。好老头布隆代对宪政政府赋予报刊的力量毫无概念；在他面前谁也不敢提那个他拒不听闻的儿子。于是，他既不知道被他咒骂过的埃米尔，也不知道埃米尔的伟大。

===Original===

Old Blondet’s integrity was as deeply rooted in him as his passion for flowers; he knew nothing but law and botany. He would have interviews with litigants, listen to them, chat with them, and show them his flowers; he would accept rare seeds from them; but once on the bench, no judge on earth was more impartial. Indeed, his manner of proceeding was so well known, that litigants never went near him except to hand over some document which might enlighten him in the performance of his duty, and nobody tried to throw dust in his eyes. With his learning, his lights, and his way of holding his real talents cheap, he was so indispensable to President du Ronceret, that, matrimonial schemes apart, that functionary would have done all that he could, in an underhand way, to prevent the vice-president from retiring in favor of his son. If the learned old man left the bench, the President would be utterly unable to do without him.
Goodman Blondet did not know that it was in Émile’s power to fulfil all his wishes in a few hours. The simplicity of his life was worthy of one of Plutarch’s men. In the evening he looked over his cases; next morning he worked among his flowers; and all day long he gave decisions on the bench. The pretty maidservant, now of ripe age, and wrinkled like an Easter pippin, looked after the house, and they lived according to the established customs of the strictest parsimony. Mlle. Cadot always carried the keys of her cupboards and fruit-loft about with her. She was indefatigable. She went to market herself, she cooked and dusted and swept, and never missed mass of a morning. To give some idea of the domestic life of the household, it will be enough to remark that the father and son never ate fruit till it was beginning to spoil, because Mlle. Cadot always brought out anything that would not keep. No one in the house ever tasted the luxury of new bread, and all the fast days in the calendar were punctually observed. The gardener was put on rations like a soldier; the elderly Valideh always kept an eye upon him. And she, for her part, was so deferentially treated, that she took her meals with the family, and in consequence was continually trotting to and fro between the kitchen and the parlor at breakfast and dinner time.
Mlle.

===Chinese===

老布隆代的正直，同他对花的痴迷一样根深蒂固；他只懂法律和植物学。他会接见诉讼当事人，听他们讲，同他们聊，领他们看他的花；他会收下他们的稀有种子；可一旦坐上审判席，天底下没有比他更公正的法官。而且他这一套行事做派人人皆知，所以当事人去找他，只为递交一份能帮助他断案的文书，谁也别想蒙他的眼。以他的学问、见识和他不把真才实学当回事的脾气，他对院长迪·龙瑟雷实在不可或缺；所以，撇开联姻的算计不谈，那位官员本会千方百计暗地里阻挠副庭长告老让位给儿子。这位博学的老人一旦离开审判席，院长将全然应付不了。
好老头布隆代不知道，埃米尔几个钟头之内便有能力满足他的一切心愿。他生活的简朴，配得上普鲁塔克笔下的人物。晚上他披阅案卷；第二天上午侍弄花草；一整天坐在审判席上裁断是非。当年那个漂亮的女仆，如今上了年纪，皱纹密布像一只复活节苹果，掌管着家务；父子俩按最严格的节俭的老规矩过活。卡多小姐随身总带着橱柜和果窖的钥匙。她不知疲倦。她亲自上市场，做饭、掸尘、扫地，每天早晨的弥撒从不缺席。要领略这户人家的家庭生活，只需说一件事就够了：父子俩吃水果，从来只吃开始变坏的，因为卡多小姐端出来的总是存放不住的那些。家里谁也没尝过新面包这种奢侈，历书上所有斋戒日都严格奉行。园丁同士兵一样领口粮；年迈的瓦利德总盯着他。而她自己呢，受到的敬重使她同主人家同桌用饭，于是早晚饭时，她便在厨房与客厅之间跑进跑出，忙个不停。
至于那位小姐

===Original===

Blandureau’s parents had consented to her marriage with Joseph Blondet upon one condition﻿—the penniless and briefless barrister must be an assistant judge. So, with the desire of fitting his son to fill the position, old M. Blondet racked his brains to hammer the law into his son’s head by dint of lessons, so as to make a cut-and-dried lawyer of him. As for Blondet junior, he spent almost every evening at the Blandureaus’ house, to which also young Fabien du Ronceret had been admitted since his return, without raising the slightest suspicion in the minds of father or son.
Everything in this life of theirs was measured with an accuracy worthy of Gérard Dou’s *Money Changer*; not a grain of salt too much, not a single profit foregone; but the economical principles by which it was regulated were relaxed in favor of the greenhouse and garden. “The garden was the master’s craze,” Mlle. Cadot used to say. The master’s blind fondness for Joseph was not a craze in her eyes; she shared the father’s predilection; she pampered Joseph; she darned his stockings; and would have been better pleased if the money spent on the garden had been put by for Joseph’s benefit.
That garden was kept in marvelous order by a single man; the paths, covered with river-sand, continually turned over with the rake, meandered among the borders full of the rarest flowers. Here were all kinds of color and scent, here were lizards on the walls, legions of little flowerpots standing out in the sun, regiments of forks and hoes, and a host of innocent things, a combination of pleasant results to justify the gardener’s charming hobby.
At the end of the greenhouse the judge had set up a grandstand, an amphitheatre of benches to hold some five or six thousand pelargoniums in pots﻿—a splendid and famous show. People came to see his geraniums in flower, not only from the neighborhood, but even from the departments round about. The Empress Marie Louise, passing through the town, had honored the curiously kept greenhouse with a visit; so much was she impressed with the sight, that she spoke of it to Napoleon, and the old judge received the Cross of the Legion of Honor.

===Chinese===

——布朗迪罗家——她的父母已答应她同约瑟夫·布隆代结婚，条件只有一个：这个身无分文又无案可办的律师必须当上助理法官。于是，老布隆代为了把儿子造就得配得上这个位置，绞尽脑汁，靠填鸭式的课业把法律往儿子脑袋里硬敲，想把他敲成一个现成的法学家。至于小布隆代，他几乎每晚都泡在布朗迪罗家；自从法比安·迪·龙瑟雷回城以来，那位也被接纳了进去，父子俩谁也没有起过半点疑心。
他们这种日子里的样样东西都掂量得丝毫不爽，配得上热拉尔·道那幅《兑换银钱的人》；盐不多一粒，利不放过一文；只是这套治家的节俭原则，对温室和花园网开一面。“花园是老爷的疯病。”卡多小姐常说。而老爷对约瑟夫的盲目偏爱，在她眼里可不是疯病；她同父亲一样偏疼这个儿子；她惯着约瑟夫，替他补袜子；花在花园里的钱若能替约瑟夫攒下来，她倒更称心。
那座园子由一个园丁收拾得井井有条；小径铺着河沙，耙子不停地翻整，在长满奇花异卉的花畦间蜿蜒。这里有色有香，墙上有蜥蜴，一队队小花盆在太阳底下晾着，一排排草叉锄头，还有许许多多无害的东西——种种赏心悦目的成果凑在一起，为这位司法官可爱的癖好作了辩护。
温室尽头，法官搭起了看台——一层层阶梯花凳，摆得下五六千盆天竺葵——一场辉煌而著名的花展。来赏他天竺葵开花的，不但有左近乡邻，甚至有邻近各郡的人。玛丽·路易丝皇后路过本城时，曾亲临这座经营得奇妙的温室；她见之大为赞叹，说与拿破仑听，老法官因此得了荣誉军团十字勋章。

===Original===

But as the learned gardener never mingled in society at all, and went nowhere except to the Blandureaus, he had no suspicion of the President’s underhand manoeuvres; and others who could see the President’s intentions were far too much afraid of him to interfere or to warn the inoffensive Blondets.
As for Michu, that young man with his powerful connections gave much more thought to making himself agreeable to the women in the upper social circles to which he was introduced by the Cinq-Cygnes, than to the extremely simple business of a provincial Tribunal. With his independent means (he had an income of twelve thousand livres), he was courted by mothers of daughters, and led a frivolous life. He did just enough at the Tribunal to satisfy his conscience, much as a schoolboy does his exercises, saying ditto on all occasions, with a “Yes, dear President.” But underneath the appearance of indifference lurked the unusual powers of the Paris law student who had distinguished himself as one of the staff of prosecuting counsel before he came to the provinces. He was accustomed to taking broad views of things; he could do rapidly what the President and Blondet could only do after much thinking, and very often solved knotty points for them. In delicate conjunctures the President and Vice-President took counsel with their junior, confided thorny questions to him, and never failed to wonder at the readiness with which he brought back a task in which old Blondet found nothing to criticise. Michu was sure of the influence of the most crabbed aristocrats, and he was young and rich; he lived, therefore, above the level of departmental intrigues and pettinesses. He was an indispensable man at picnics, he frisked with young ladies and paid court to their mothers, he danced at balls, he gambled like a capitalist. In short, he played his part of young lawyer of fashion to admiration; without, at the same time, compromising his dignity, which he knew how to assert at the right moment like a man of spirit. He won golden opinions by the manner in which he threw himself into provincial ways, without criticising them; and for these reasons, everyone endeavored to make his time of exile endurable.
The public prosecutor was a lawyer of the highest ability; he had taken the plunge into political life, and was one of the most distinguished speakers on the ministerialist benches.

===Chinese===

可是这位博学的园艺家根本不与社交界往来，除了布朗迪罗家哪儿也不去，所以对院长的暗中兴风作浪毫无察觉；而那些看得清院长意图的人，又太怕他，谁也不肯出面干预，或给这家人畜无害的布隆代父子提个醒。
至于米许，这个有硬靠山的青年，心思十之八九用在讨上层社交圈的太太小姐们的欢心上——他是被桑-赛涅家引进那个圈子的——用在初等法院那点极简单的公务上的，十不及一。他有自己的进项（年金1.2万里弗尔），做母亲们的争相奉承，日子过得轻浮。他在法院里的作为，刚好够安抚良心，活像个做功课的小学生，事事随声附和，一句“是，亲爱的院长”。可是在这淡漠的外表底下，藏着巴黎法学院学生不寻常的功力——来外省之前，他已在公诉官署任上崭露头角。他习惯于大处着眼；院长和布隆代要苦思冥想半天的事，他一挥而就，还常常替他们解开死结。遇到微妙的关头，院长和副庭长便同这位下属商量，把棘手的难题托付给他，事后无不惊叹他交差的利落，连老布隆代也挑不出毛病。米许有最别扭的贵族们的影响作保，又年轻又有钱；因此他活得高出郡里的阴谋与琐碎。野餐会上他是少不得的人物；他同小姐们嬉闹，向她们的母亲献殷勤；他在舞会上跳舞；他像资本家一样豪赌。总之，他把时髦青年律师的角色演得出神入化，同时又不失体面——他知道像个有血性的人那样，在恰当的时刻端出尊严。他全身心投入外省的一套风俗而不加批评，因此处处赢得金口玉言；也正因如此，人人都设法让他这段谪居的日子好过一些。
公诉人是一位才具极高的法学家；他已一头扎进政治生活，是内阁派议席上最出色的演说家之一。

===Original===

The President stood in awe of him; if he had not been away in Paris at the time, no steps would have been taken against Victurnien; his dexterity, his experience of business, would have prevented the whole affair. At that moment, however, he was in the Chamber of Deputies, and the President and du Croisier had taken advantage of his absence to weave their plot, calculating, with a certain ingenuity, that if once the law stepped in, and the matter was noised abroad, things would have gone too far to be remedied.
As a matter of fact, no staff of prosecuting counsel in any Tribunal, at that particular time, would have taken up a charge of forgery against the eldest son of one of the noblest houses in France without going into the case at great length, and a special reference, in all probability, to the Attorney-General. In such a case as this, the authorities and the Government would have tried endless ways of compromising and hushing up an affair which might send an imprudent young man to the hulks. They would very likely have done the same for a Liberal family in a prominent position, so long as the Liberals were not too openly hostile to the throne and the altar. So du Croisier’s charge and the young Count’s arrest had not been very easy to manage. The President and du Croisier had compassed their ends in the following manner.
M. Sauvager, a young Royalist barrister, had reached the position of deputy public prosecutor by dint of subservience to the Ministry. In the absence of his chief he was head of the staff of counsel for prosecution, and, consequently, it fell to him to take up the charge made by du Croisier. Sauvager was a self-made man; he had nothing but his stipend; and for that reason the authorities reckoned upon someone who had everything to gain by devotion. The President now exploited the position. No sooner was the document with the alleged forgery in du Croisier’s hands, than Mme. la Présidente du Ronceret, prompted by her spouse, had a long conversation with M. Sauvager. In the course of it she pointed out the uncertainties of a career in the *magistrature debout* compared with the *magistrature assise*, and the advantages of the bench over the bar; she showed how a freak on the part of some official, or a single false step, might ruin a man’s career.

===Chinese===

院长对他敬畏三分；倘若他当时不是人在巴黎，谁也不会对维克图尼安下手；他的机变、他办事的老练，会把整件事按住。可是那时他正在众议院，院长和杜·克鲁瓦西耶趁他不在织好了罗网，还带着几分巧思算准：只要法律一插手，事情一张扬出去，便再无法收拾。
事实上，在那个当口，无论哪家法院的公诉官署，要对法兰西最显赫世家之一的长子接下一桩伪造汇票的控告，不把案子反复掂量、多半还要专门请示总检察长，是绝无可能的。遇到这种案子，官府和政府会想尽办法私了、压下——这案子可能把一个轻率的青年送上苦役船。只要自由党人同王座与圣坛的敌对不太露骨，对一个地位显赫的自由党人家，他们多半也会同样办理。所以，杜·克鲁瓦西耶的控告和年轻伯爵的被捕，办起来并不容易。院长和杜·克鲁瓦西耶是靠下面这套办法达到目的的。
索瓦热先生，一个年轻的保王党律师，靠对朝廷百依百顺爬到了副公诉人的位子。上司不在，他便主持公诉官署，于是接下杜·克鲁瓦西耶的控告，非他莫属。索瓦热是白手起家；他只有薪水可恃；正因为如此，当局才看中了这个靠效忠就能换来一切的人。院长如今利用的正是这一点。载有所谓伪造罪证的那张票据一到杜·克鲁瓦西耶手里，院长夫人迪·龙瑟雷便奉夫命同索瓦热先生作了一次长谈。谈话中，她指出“站着的司法官”（检控官）比起“坐着的司法官”（审判官）前程何等不定，法官又比律师有多少好处；她讲明，哪位大人物的一时兴起，或者一步失足，就可能断送一个人的前程。

===Original===

“If you are conscientious and give your conclusions against the powers that be, you are lost,” continued she. “Now, at this moment, you might turn your position to account to make a fine match that would put you above unlucky chances for the rest of your life; you may marry a wife with fortune sufficient to land you on the bench, in the *magistrature assise*. There is a fine chance for you. M. du Croisier will never have any children; everybody knows why. His money, and his wife’s as well, will go to his niece, Mlle. Duval. M. Duval is an ironmaster, his purse is tolerably filled, to begin with, and his father is still alive, and has a little property besides. The father and son have a million of francs between them; they will double it with du Croisier’s help, for du Croisier has business connections among great capitalists and manufacturers in Paris. M. and Mme. Duval the younger would be certain to give their daughter to a suitor brought forward by du Croisier, for he is sure to leave two fortunes to his niece; and, in all probability, he will settle the reversion of his wife’s property upon Mlle. Duval in the marriage contract, for Mme. du Croisier has no kin. You know how du Croisier hates the d’Esgrignons. Do him a service, be his man, take up this charge of forgery which he is going to make against young d’Esgrignon, and follow up the proceedings at once without consulting the public prosecutor at Paris. And, then, pray Heaven that the Ministry dismisses you for doing your office impartially, in spite of the powers that be; for if they do, your fortune is made! You will have a charming wife and thirty thousand francs a year with her, to say nothing of four millions expectations in ten years’ time.”
In two evenings Sauvager was talked over. Both he and the President kept the affair a secret from old Blondet, from Michu, and from the second member of the staff of prosecuting counsel. Feeling sure of Blondet’s impartiality on a question of fact, the President made certain of a majority without counting Camusot. And now Camusot’s unexpected defection had thrown everything out. What the President wanted was a committal for trial before the public prosecutor got warning. How if Camusot or the second counsel for the prosecution should send word to Paris?

===Chinese===

“您若凭良心办事，结论同当权者作对，您就完了。”她接着说，“可是眼下，您可以借着自己这个位置结一门好亲，从此一生不再受命运的播弄；您可以娶一位陪嫁足够的太太，稳稳登上审判席。好机会来了。杜·克鲁瓦西耶先生不会有什么孩子；缘故人人皆知。他的钱，连同他太太的钱，都将归他的内侄女迪瓦尔小姐。迪瓦尔先生是铁厂老板，钱包先就相当充实，他父亲又还在世，另有一小份产业。父子俩合起来有100万法郎；靠杜·克鲁瓦西耶帮忙，还能翻上一倍，因为杜·克鲁瓦西耶同巴黎的大资本家和厂主都有生意往来。小迪瓦尔先生和夫人准会把女儿嫁给杜·克鲁瓦西耶保举的求婚者，因为他注定要把两份家产留给内侄女；而且十之八九，他会在婚书里把妻子的财产继承权指定给迪瓦尔小姐，因为杜·克鲁瓦西耶夫人没有亲人。您知道杜·克鲁瓦西耶多恨埃斯格里尼翁家。为他效一次劳，做他的人，把他对小德·埃斯格里尼翁就要提出的伪造罪控告接过来，立即跟进诉讼程序，不必请示巴黎的公诉人。然后，求上天保佑内阁因为您不顾当权者秉公办差而撤您的职；因为真撤了，您的富贵就到手了！您会有一位娇妻，随妻带来年入3万法郎，更不说十年之内400万法郎的指望了。”
两个晚上，索瓦热就被说动了。他和院长把这件事瞒得铁桶也似，瞒着老布隆代，瞒着米许，也瞒着公诉官署的第二位属员。料定布隆代在事实问题上必持公正，院长不把卡缪索计算在内，也稳拿了多数。可如今卡缪索的意外倒戈，把全盘搅乱了。院长要的是：赶在公诉人得到风声之前，把案子裁定移送审判。倘若卡缪索或那位第二公诉属员给巴黎捎了信呢？

===Original===

And here some portion of Camusot’s private history may perhaps explain how it came to pass that Chesnel took it for granted that the examining magistrate would be on the d’Esgrignons’ side, and how he had the boldness to tamper in the open street with that representative of justice.
Camusot’s father, a well-known silk mercer in the Rue des Bourdonnais, was ambitious for the only son of his first marriage, and brought him up to the law. When Camusot junior took a wife, he gained with her the influence of an usher of the Royal cabinet, backstairs influence, it is true, but still sufficient, since it had brought him his first appointment as justice of the peace, and the second as examining magistrate. At the time of his marriage, his father only settled an income of six thousand francs upon him (the amount of his mother’s fortune, which he could legally claim), and as Mlle. Thirion brought him no more than twenty thousand francs as her portion, the young couple knew the hardships of hidden poverty. The salary of a provincial justice of the peace does not exceed fifteen hundred francs, while an examining magistrate’s stipend is augmented by something like a thousand francs, because his position entails expenses and extra work. The post, therefore, is much coveted, though it is not permanent, and the work is heavy, and that was why Mme. Camusot had just scolded her husband for allowing the President to read his thoughts.
Marie Cécile Amélie Thirion, after three years of marriage, perceived the blessing of Heaven upon it in the regularity of two auspicious events﻿—the births of a girl and a boy; but she prayed to be less blessed in the future. A few more of such blessings would turn straitened means into distress. M. Camusot’s father’s money was not likely to come to them for a long time; and, rich as he was, he would scarcely leave more than eight or ten thousand francs a year to each of his children, four in number, for he had been married twice. And besides, by the time that all “expectations,” as matchmakers call them, were realized, would not the magistrate have children of his own to settle in life? Anyone can imagine the situation for a little woman with plenty of sense and determination, and Mme. Camusot was such a woman. She did not refrain from meddling in matters judicial.

===Chinese===

这里交代一段卡缪索的私史，或许可以说明：谢内尔凭什么断定预审法官会站在埃斯格里尼翁家一边，又怎么敢在大街上公然去拉拢这位司法官员。
卡缪索的父亲是布尔多内街上有名的绸缎商，对前妻生的独子期望甚殷，让他学了法律。小卡缪索娶妻时，随妻子带来一位王家内室引座官的门路——后楼梯上的势力，固然是后楼梯，却也够用：他当治安法官是靠它，当预审法官还是靠它。成婚时，父亲只给他立了6000法郎的年金（那是他母亲的财产，他依法可得的份额），而蒂里翁小姐的陪嫁不过2万法郎，小两口便尝到了人不察知的清贫滋味。外省治安法官的薪俸不过1500法郎；预审官的薪俸加上1000法郎上下，因为这份差事既破费又多操劳。所以这个职位尽管不终身、差事繁重，仍叫人垂涎；这也正是卡缪索夫人刚刚责备丈夫的缘故——怪他让院长看穿了自己的心思。
玛丽·塞西尔·阿梅莉·蒂里翁结婚三年，从一女一男两桩喜事按期而至中看出上天对这桩婚姻的祝福；可她祈求上天使她往后少受些祝福。这样的祝福再多几回，拮据就要变成窘迫了。卡缪索先生父亲的钱，一时半会儿到不了他们手里；再说，老人家再有钱，给四个孩子（他结过两次婚）每人留下的，也不过每年八九千到1万法郎。何况，等到媒婆们所谓的“指望”统统兑现的时候，这位法官难道不也要有自己的一群儿女要安置了吗？一个头脑充足、性子果断的小妇人处于何种境地，人人都能想见；卡缪索夫人正是这样的女人。司法上的事，她照样插手。

===Original===

She had far too strong a sense of the gravity of a false step in her husband’s career.
She was the only child of an old servant of Louis XVIII, a valet who had followed his master in his wanderings in Italy, Courland, and England, till after the Restoration the King awarded him with the one place that he could fill at Court, and made him usher by rotation to the royal cabinet. So in Amélie’s home there had been, as it were, a sort of reflection of the Court. Thirion used to tell her about the lords, and ministers, and great men whom he announced and introduced and saw passing to and fro. The girl, brought up at the gates of the Tuileries, had caught some tincture of the maxims practised there, and adopted the dogma of passive obedience to authority. She had sagely judged that her husband, by ranging himself on the side of the d’Esgrignons, would find favor with Mme. la Duchesse de Maufrigneuse, and with two powerful families on whose influence with the King the Sieur Thirion could depend at an opportune moment. Camusot might get an appointment at the first opportunity within the jurisdiction of Paris, and afterwards at Paris itself. That promotion, dreamed of and longed for at every moment, was certain to have a salary of six thousand francs attached to it, as well as the alleviation of living in her own father’s house, or under the Camusots’ roof, and all the advantages of a father’s fortune on either side. If the adage, “Out of sight is out of mind,” holds good of most women, it is particularly true where family feeling or royal or ministerial patronage is concerned. The personal attendants of kings prosper at all times; you take an interest in a man, be it only a man in livery, if you see him every day.
Mme. Camusot, regarding herself as a bird of passage, had taken a little house in the Rue du Cygne. Furnished lodgings there were none; the town was not enough of a thoroughfare, and the Camusots could not afford to live at an inn like M. Michu. So the fair Parisian had no choice for it but to take such furniture as she could find; and as she paid a very moderate rent, the house was remarkably ugly, albeit a certain quaintness of detail was not wanting.

===Chinese===

对丈夫前程上失一步的分量，她的感觉太敏锐了。
她是路易十八一名老仆的独生女；那老仆是个贴身侍从，跟随主子辗转意大利、库尔兰和英格兰，复辟之后，国王赏给他宫里唯一胜任的差事，派他轮值王家内室的引座官。所以在阿梅莉家里，可以说有一道宫廷的反光。蒂里翁常对她讲起那些通报、引见、迎来送往的王公、大臣和显贵。这姑娘在杜伊勒里宫门口长大，染上了那里行事准则的几分底色，信奉对权威消极服从的信条。她精明地判定：丈夫站到埃斯格里尼翁家一边，就会博得德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人的欢心，还攀上两个有权势的家族——一旦时机到来，蒂里翁先生指望得上他们对国王的影响。卡缪索说不定头一个机会就能调到巴黎辖区的法院，随后调进巴黎城里。那桩升迁，是她时刻梦想渴求的，照例连着6000法郎的薪俸，还能住回自己父亲的家、或卡缪索家的屋檐下，两边父亲的财产好处尽得。俗话说“眼不见，心不念”，这话对多数女人成立，对亲情或王室、大臣的恩宠尤其成立。国王的贴身侍从任何时候都吃得开；一个人，哪怕只是个穿号衣的，你天天见，就会关心他。
卡缪索夫人把自己看作一只过路的候鸟，在天鹅街租了一所小房子。带家具的公寓这里没有；这座城算不上通衢大邑，而卡缪索家又住不起米许先生那样的客栈。于是这位巴黎美人别无选择，只能置办些找得到的家具；房租付得极低，房子也就丑得出奇，虽然细处倒也不乏几分古趣。

===Original===

It was built against a neighboring house in such a fashion that the side, with only one window in each story, gave upon the street, and the front looked out upon a yard where rosebushes and buckhorn were growing along the wall on either side. On the farther side, opposite the house, stood a shed, a roof over two brick arches. A little wicket-gate gave entrance into the gloomy place (made gloomier still by the great walnut-tree which grew in the yard), and a double flight of steps, with an elaborately-wrought but rust-eaten handrail, led to the house door. Inside the house there were two rooms on each floor. The dining-room occupied that part of the ground floor nearest the street, and the kitchen lay on the other side of a narrow passage almost wholly taken up by the wooden staircase. Of the two first-floor rooms, one did duty as the magistrate’s study, the other as a bedroom, while the nursery and the servants’ bedroom stood above in the attics. There were no ceilings in the house; the crossbeams were simply whitewashed and the spaces plastered over. Both rooms on the first floor and the dining-room below were wainscoted and adorned with the labyrinthine designs which taxed the patience of the eighteenth century joiner; but the carving had been painted a dingy gray most depressing to behold.
The magistrate’s study looked as though it belonged to a provincial lawyer; it contained a big bureau, a mahogany armchair, a law student’s books, and shabby belongings transported from Paris. Mme. Camusot’s room was more of a native product; it boasted a blue-and-white scheme of decoration, a carpet, and that anomalous kind of furniture which appears to be in the fashion, while it is simply some style that has failed in Paris. As to the dining-room, it was nothing but an ordinary provincial dining-room, bare and chilly, with a damp, faded paper on the walls.
In this shabby room, with nothing to see but the walnut-tree, the dark leaves growing against the walls, and the almost deserted road beyond them, a somewhat lively and frivolous woman, accustomed to the amusements and stir of Paris, used to sit all day long, day after day, and for the most part of the time alone, though she received tiresome and inane visits which led her to think her loneliness preferable to empty tittle-tattle.

===Chinese===

房子紧贴着邻宅修建，结果只有侧面临街，每层开一扇窗；正面朝一个小院，院墙两侧长着玫瑰和鹿角漆树。院子对面搭着一座棚屋，两个砖拱上架一面屋顶。一扇小边门通进这块幽暗的地方（院里那棵大胡桃树使它更显幽暗），两道石阶通向正门，铁栏杆雕工繁复，却已锈烂。屋里每层两间房。餐厅占着底层靠街的那一侧；厨房在一条窄过道另一头，过道几乎被木楼梯占满。二楼两间，一间充法官的书房，另一间作卧室；育儿室和女仆的卧室在上面阁楼里。屋里没有天花板；横梁只刷了白灰，梁间抹了灰泥。二楼两间连同楼下餐厅都镶着护壁板，饰着十八世纪细木工磨尽耐性的迷宫纹样；只是雕花被漆成了一种看着叫人丧气的暗灰色。
法官的书房一副外省律师的气派：一张大写字台，一把桃花心木圈椅，一个法科学生的藏书，还有从巴黎运来的破旧家什。卡缪索夫人的房间则更像本地出品：蓝白两色的装饰格调，一块地毯，还有那种说不清的家具——看着时髦，其实不过是巴黎过了气的式样。至于餐厅，就是普普通通一间外省饭厅，空落清冷，墙上糊着受潮褪色的墙纸。
在这间寒酸的屋子里，看得见的只有那棵胡桃树、贴着院墙的深色树叶和墙外几乎无人行走的道路；一个活泼好动、过惯了巴黎的热闹与消遣的女人，整日整日坐在这里，而且多半独自一人，尽管也接待些乏味空洞的来访——那些来访倒叫她觉得，孤独还胜过空空洞洞的闲磕牙。

===Original===

If she permitted herself the slightest gleam of intelligence, it gave rise to interminable comment and embittered her condition. She occupied herself a great deal with her children, not so much from taste as for the sake of an interest in her almost solitary life, and exercised her mind on the only subjects which she could find﻿—to wit, the intrigues which went on around her, the ways of provincials, and the ambitions shut in by their narrow horizons. So she very soon fathomed mysteries of which her husband had no idea. As she sat at her window with a piece of intermittent embroidery work in her fingers, she did not see her woodshed full of faggots nor the servant busy at the wash tub; she was looking out upon Paris, Paris where everything is pleasure, everything is full of life. She dreamed of Paris gaieties, and shed tears because she must abide in this dull prison of a country town. She was disconsolate because she lived in a peaceful district, where no conspiracy, no great affair would ever occur. She saw herself doomed to sit under the shadow of the walnut-tree for some time to come.
Mme. Camusot was a little, plump, fresh, fair-haired woman, with a very prominent forehead, a mouth which receded, and a turned-up chin, a type of countenance which is passable in youth, but looks old before the time. Her bright, quick eyes expressed her innocent desire to get on in the world, and the envy born of her present inferior position, with rather too much candor; but still they lighted up her commonplace face and set it off with a certain energy of feeling, which success was certain to extinguish in later life. At that time she used to give a good deal of time and thought to her dresses, inventing trimmings and embroidering them; she planned out her costumes with the maid whom she had brought with her from Paris, and so maintained the reputation of Parisiennes in the provinces. Her caustic tongue was dreaded; she was not loved. In that keen, investigating spirit peculiar to unoccupied women who are driven to find some occupation for empty days, she had pondered the President’s private opinions, until at length she discovered what he meant to do, and for some time past she had advised Camusot to declare war. The young Count’s affair was an excellent opportunity.

===Chinese===

她只要稍稍露出一点灵光的锋芒，就会招来没完没了的议论，使她的处境更苦。她把大量心思花在孩子身上，与其说是天性使然，不如说是为这份近乎孤寂的生活找一个寄托；她拿身边仅有的题目锻炼头脑——也就是四周进行的种种阴谋、外省人的做派，和困在狭窄视野里的野心。于是她很快就参透了她丈夫毫无概念的种种机密。她坐在窗前，手里做着一针一阵的刺绣活；她看不见自家堆满柴捆的柴棚，也看不见在洗衣桶边忙活的女仆；她望的是巴黎，那个一切都乐趣、一切都生气勃勃的巴黎。她梦想巴黎的欢乐，为自己不得不困守这座沉闷的外省牢城落泪。她灰心丧气，因为住在一个人畜无害的太平地方，永远不会出什么阴谋、什么大案。她眼看自己命定还要在那棵胡桃树的荫翳底下坐上好一阵子。
卡缪索夫人是个娇小丰润、皮肤鲜嫩的金发女人，额头很凸，嘴巴后缩，下巴上翘——这种脸型年轻时还看得过去，可老得早。她那双明快伶俐的眼睛，把她往上爬的天真心愿和眼下次等地位生出的嫉妒，表达得未免太露骨；不过它们总算点亮了她那张平常的脸，添上一股情感的劲头——这份劲头，日后的成功必将把它熄灭。那时候，她在衣着上费很多心思和时光，自己发明花边饰带再亲手绣出来；她同从巴黎带来的女仆一起筹划装束，就这样在外省撑住了巴黎女人的名声。她那张刻薄的嘴叫人害怕；她不讨人喜欢。凭着赋闲女人才有的那种锐气——闲得发慌，只好替空落落的日子找点事干——她把院长的私人观点掂量来掂量去，终于识破了他的意图；一段时间以来，她一直劝卡缪索宣战。年轻伯爵的案子，正是一个绝好的机会。

===Original===

Was it not obviously Camusot’s part to make a stepping-stone of this criminal case by favoring the d’Esgrignons, a family with power of a very different kind from the power of the du Croisier party?
“Sauvager will never marry Mlle. Duval. They are dangling her before him, but he will be the dupe of those Machiavels in the Val-Noble to whom he is going to sacrifice his position. Camusot, this affair, so unfortunate as it is for the d’Esgrignons, so insidiously brought on by the President for du Croisier’s benefit, will turn out well for nobody but *you*,” she had said, as they went in.
The shrewd Parisienne had likewise guessed the President’s underhand manoeuvres with the Blandureaus, and his object in baffling old Blondet’s efforts, but she saw nothing to be gained by opening the eyes of father or son to the perils of the situation; she was enjoying the beginning of the comedy; she knew about the proposals made by Chesnel’s successor on behalf of Fabien du Ronceret, but she did not suspect how important that secret might be to her. If she or her husband were threatened by the President, Mme. Camusot could threaten too, in her turn, to call the amateur gardener’s attention to a scheme for carrying off the flower which he meant to transplant into his house.
Chesnel had not penetrated, like Mme. Camusot, into the means by which Sauvager had been won over; but by dint of looking into the various lives and interests of the men grouped about the Lilies of the Tribunal, he knew that he could count upon the public prosecutor, upon Camusot, and M. Michu. Two judges for the d’Esgrignons would paralyze the rest. And, finally, Chesnel knew old Blondet well enough to feel sure that if he ever swerved from impartiality, it would be for the sake of the work of his whole lifetime﻿—to secure his son’s appointment. So Chesnel slept, full of confidence, on the resolve to go to M. Blondet and offer to realize his so long cherished hopes, while he opened his eyes to President du Ronceret’s treachery. Blondet won over, he would take a peremptory tone with the examining magistrate, to whom he hoped to prove that if Victurnien was not blameless, he had been merely imprudent; the whole thing should be shown in the light of a boy’s thoughtless escapade.
But Chesnel slept neither soundly nor for long. Before dawn he was awakened by his housekeeper.

===Chinese===

帮埃斯格里尼翁家一把，把这件刑案当垫脚石，岂不明摆着是卡缪索该扮演的角色？这个家族的势力，同杜·克鲁瓦西耶一党的势力可完全是两码事。
“索瓦热永远娶不上迪瓦尔小姐。她们拿她在他眼前晃来晃去，可他只会被瓦勒-诺布尔那些马基雅维利们耍弄——他正要为这些人牺牲自己的前程。卡缪索，这桩案子，对埃斯格里尼翁家固然是大不幸，又是院长为杜·克鲁瓦西耶的利益阴险促成的，到头来对谁都没好处——除了你。”进门的时候她这样说过。
这位精明的巴黎女人同样猜到了院长对布朗迪罗家的暗中运作，猜到他阻挠老布隆代的用意；但她看不出点破这对父子的险境有什么好处；她正欣赏着这出喜剧的开场；她知道谢内尔的继任者替法比安·迪·龙瑟雷提过亲，可她没想到那个秘密对她会有多要紧。万一她或丈夫受到院长的威胁，卡缪索夫人也可以反过来威胁：提醒那位业余园艺家，有人正在打那朵他打算移进自家花房的花的主意。
索瓦热是被什么手段收买的，谢内尔没有像卡缪索夫人那样看透；但凭着把法院百合花徽周围这些人的身世与利害一一参详，他知道可以指望公诉人，指望卡缪索和米许先生。有两位法官向着埃斯格里尼翁家，其余的人就动弹不得。最后，谢内尔对老布隆代知之甚深，确信他若有偏离公正的一天，那也必是为了他毕生的心事——儿子的任命。于是谢内尔满怀信心地睡下，拿定主意明天去找布隆代先生，提出替他了却那桩夙愿，同时让他看清院长迪·龙瑟雷的背信。收服了布隆代，他就可以用不容分说的口吻对付预审法官，向他证明：维克图尼安纵非清白无辜，也不过是轻率；整件事只应看作一个孩子没心眼的胡闹。
可是谢内尔睡得既不沉，也不长。天亮之前，女管家把他叫醒了。

===Original===

The most bewitching person in this history, the most adorable youth on the face of the globe, Mme. la Duchesse de Maufrigneuse herself, in man’s attire, had driven alone from Paris in a calèche, and was waiting to see him.
“I have come to save him or to die with him,” said she, addressing the notary, who thought that he was dreaming. “I have brought a hundred thousand francs, given me by His Majesty out of his private purse, to buy Victurnien’s innocence, if his adversary can be bribed. If we fail utterly, I have brought poison to snatch him away before anything takes place, before even the indictment is drawn up. But we shall not fail. I have sent word to the public prosecutor; he is on the road behind me; he could not travel in my calèche, because he wished to take the instructions of the Keeper of the Seals.”
Chesnel rose to the occasion and played up to the Duchess; he wrapped himself in his dressing-gown, fell at her feet, and kissed them, not without asking her pardon for forgetting himself in his joy.
“We are saved!” cried he; and gave orders to Brigitte to see that Mme. la Duchesse had all that she needed after traveling post all night. He appealed to the fair Diane’s spirit, by making her see that it was absolutely necessary that she should visit the examining magistrate before daylight, lest anyone should discover the secret, or so much as imagine that the Duchesse de Maufrigneuse had come.
“And have I not a passport in due form?” quoth she, displaying a sheet of paper, wherein she was described as M. le Vicomte Félix de Vandeness, Master of Requests, and His Majesty’s private secretary. “And do I not play my man’s part well?” she added, running her fingers through her wig à la Titus, and twirling her riding switch.
“O! Mme. la Duchesse, you are an angel!” cried Chesnel, with tears in his eyes. (She was destined always to be an angel, even in man’s attire.) “Button up your greatcoat, muffle yourself up to the eyes in your traveling cloak, take my arm, and let us go as quickly as possible to Camusot’s house before anybody can meet us.”
“Then am I going to see a man called Camusot?” she asked.
“With a nose to match his name,”7 assented Chesnel.

这部故事里最迷人的角色、全球最可爱的青年——德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人本人，一身男装，独自乘着一辆双篷四轮马车从巴黎赶来，正在等他。
“我来救他，不然就同他一起死。”她对公证人说；公证人只当自己在做梦。“我带来10万法郎，是陛下从私囊里拿出来的，用来买维克图尼安的无罪——如果对头肯受贿的话。倘若一败涂地，我还带着毒药，要在事情发动之前、连起诉书还没拟出来之前，就把他抢走。可我们不会败。我已经通知了公诉人；他在我后面路上；他不便同我共乘一车，因为他要先领受掌玺大臣的指示。”
谢内尔应付得体，与公爵夫人配合默契；他裹上晨衣，拜倒在她脚下，吻她的脚，还不忘为自己的失态请罪——那是欢喜过了头。
“我们得救了！”他喊道；随即吩咐布丽吉特，务必让公爵夫人在连夜赶驿之后样样舒齐。他唤起美丽的迪安娜的豪情，让她明白：必须赶在天亮之前去见预审法官，免得任何人发现这个秘密，甚至连德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人来过此地这个念头，也不能让人起。
“再说，我这不是有手续齐备的护照么？”她说着，亮出一纸文书，上面写她是费利克斯·德·汪德奈斯子爵先生，行政法院查案官，陛下私人秘书。“我这个男人扮得像不像？”她补了一句，手指插进提图斯式的假发里拢了拢，转着手里的马鞭。
“哦！公爵夫人，您是一位天使！”谢内尔眼里含着泪喊道。（她注定永远是天使，连男装也改变不了。）“扣上大衣，拿旅行斗篷把脸裹到眼睛，挽住我的胳膊，我们尽快去卡缪索家，赶在碰上任何人之前。”
“这么说，我要去见一个叫卡缪索的人？”她问。
“鼻子同名字正相配。”7谢内尔附和道。

===Original===

The old notary felt his heart dead within him, but he thought it none the less necessary to humor the Duchess, to laugh when she laughed, and shed tears when she wept; groaning in spirit, all the same, over the feminine frivolity which could find matter for a jest while setting about a matter so serious. What would he not have done to save the Count? While Chesnel dressed; Mme. de Maufrigneuse sipped the cup of coffee and cream which Brigitte brought her, and agreed with herself that provincial women cooks are superior to Parisian chefs, who despise the little details which make all the difference to an epicure. Thanks to Chesnel’s taste for delicate fare, Brigitte was found prepared to set an excellent meal before the Duchess.
Chesnel and his charming companion set out for M. and Mme. Camusot’s house.
“Ah! so there is a Mme. Camusot?” said the Duchess. “Then the affair may be managed.”
“And so much the more readily, because the lady is visibly tired enough of living among us provincials; she comes from Paris,” said Chesnel.
“Then we must have no secrets from her?”
“You will judge how much to tell or to conceal,” Chesnel replied humbly. “I am sure that she will be greatly flattered to be the Duchesse de Maufrigneuse’s hostess; you will be obliged to stay in her house until nightfall, I expect, unless you find it inconvenient to remain.”
“Is this Mme. Camusot a good-looking woman?” asked the Duchess, with a coxcomb’s air.
“She is a bit of a queen in her own house.”
“Then she is sure to meddle in courthouse affairs,” returned the Duchess. “Nowhere but in France, my dear M. Chesnel, do you see women so much wedded to their husbands that they are wedded to their husband’s professions, work, or business as well. In Italy, England, and Germany, women make it a point of honor to leave men to fight their own battles; they shut their eyes to their husbands’ work as perseveringly as our French citizens’ wives do all that in them lies to understand the position of their joint-stock partnership; is not that what you call it in your legal language? Frenchwomen are so incredibly jealous in the conduct of their married life, that they insist on knowing everything; and that is how, in the least difficulty, you feel the wife’s hand in the business; the Frenchwoman advises, guides, and warns her husband. And, truth to tell, the man is none the worse off.

老公证人觉得自己的心都死了，可他认为照样有必要顺着公爵夫人的兴致：她笑他也笑，她哭他也落泪，心里却为这份女性轻浮暗暗叫苦——事情这样性命交关，她竟还能找出打趣的题目。为了救伯爵，他有什么不肯做的呢？谢内尔穿衣裳的当口，德·莫弗里涅斯夫人小口啜着布丽吉特端来的奶油咖啡，一面暗自认定：外省的厨娘胜过巴黎的名厨，后者看不起那些细节，而美食家的成败全在细节。亏得谢内尔讲究口腹，布丽吉特随时拿得出手，给公爵夫人端上一桌极好的饭食。
谢内尔同他这位可爱的同伴，动身去卡缪索先生和夫人的家。
“啊！还有一位卡缪索夫人？”公爵夫人说，“那么事情有门了。”
“而且好办得多，因为这位夫人分明是同我们外省人过腻了；她是巴黎人。”谢内尔说。
“那我们对她就不用保密了？”
“说什么、瞒什么，您自己斟酌。”谢内尔谦恭地答道，“我敢肯定，做德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人的东道，她会受宠若惊；我想您只好在她家躲到天黑，除非您觉得待着不便。”
“这位卡缪索夫人长得漂亮吗？”公爵夫人摆出纨绔少年的架子问。
“在自己家里，她算半个王后。”
“那她一定插手法院的事务。”公爵夫人答道，“亲爱的谢内尔先生，只有在法兰西，你才能见到女人这样嫁给了丈夫，连丈夫的职业、差事、买卖也一并嫁了。在意大利、英国和德国，女人把让男人自己打自己的仗引为体面；她们对丈夫的营生闭眼不看，锲而不舍，一如我们法国市民的妻子锲而不舍地要弄清自家合股生意的底细——你们法律上的行话是这么叫的吧？法兰西女人操持起婚姻生活来，醋劲大得难以置信，样样都要知道；所以哪怕出一点小乱子，你都能摸到生意里那只妻子的手；法兰西女人给丈夫出主意、指方向、打招呼。说句实话，男人倒也不吃亏。

In England, if a married man is put in prison for debt for twenty-four hours, his wife will be jealous and make a scene when he comes back.”
“Here we are, without meeting a soul on the way,” said Chesnel. “You are the more sure of complete ascendency here, Mme. la Duchesse, since Mme. Camusot’s father is one Thirion, usher of the royal cabinet.”
“And the King never thought of that!” exclaimed the Duchess. “He thinks of nothing! Thirion introduced us, the Prince de Cadignan, M. de Vandeness, and me! We shall have it all our own way in this house. Settle everything with M. Camusot while I talk to his wife.”
The maid, who was washing and dressing the children, showed the visitors into the little fireless dining-room.
“Take that card to your mistress,” said the Duchess, lowering her voice for the woman’s ear; “nobody else is to see it. If you are discreet, child, you shall not lose by it.”
At the sound of a woman’s voice, and the sight of the handsome young man’s face, the maid looked thunderstruck.
“Wake M. Camusot,” said Chesnel, “and tell him, that I am waiting to see him on important business,” and she departed upstairs forthwith.
A few minutes later Mme. Camusot, in her dressing-gown, sprang downstairs and brought the handsome stranger into her room. She had pushed Camusot out of bed and into his study with all his clothes, bidding him dress himself at once and wait there. The transformation scene had been brought about by a bit of pasteboard with the words **Madame la Duchesse de Maufrigneuse** engraved upon it. A daughter of the usher of the royal cabinet took in the whole situation at once.
“Well!” exclaimed the maidservant, left with Chesnel in the dining-room, “Would not anyone think that a thunderbolt had dropped in among us? The master is dressing in his study; you can go upstairs.”
“Not a word of all this, mind,” said Chesnel.
Now that he was conscious of the support of a great lady who had the King’s consent (by word of mouth) to the measures about to be taken for rescuing the Comte d’Esgrignon, he spoke with an air of authority, which served his cause much better with Camusot than the humility with which he would otherwise have approached him.
“Sir,” said he, “the words let fall last evening may have surprised you, but they are serious.

“在英国，一个有妇之夫因债务坐上二十四个小时的监牢，太太还要吃醋，等他回来大闹一场呢。”
“到了，一路上一个人影也没碰上。”谢内尔说，“公爵夫人，您在这家里更有十足把握了，因为卡缪索夫人的父亲，正是那位蒂里翁，王家内室的引座官。”
“国王竟没想到这一层！”公爵夫人喊道，“他什么都不想！当年给我们引见的就是蒂里翁——卡迪尼昂亲王、德·汪德奈斯先生，还有我！在这户人家里，我们爱怎样就怎样。您同卡缪索先生谈正事，我去做他太太的工作。”
女仆正给孩子们梳洗穿衣，把两位来客让进那间没有生火的小餐厅。
“把这张名片送给你的女主人，”公爵夫人压低嗓音对她说，“别的人一概不许看。你嘴严的话，孩子，少不了你的好处。”
听见女人的嗓音，又看见那位俊俏青年的脸，女仆惊得目瞪口呆。
“叫醒卡缪索先生，”谢内尔说，“告诉他，我有要事见他。”女仆随即上楼去了。
几分钟后，卡缪索夫人披着晨衣，噔噔噔跑下楼来，把俊俏的生客领进了自己的房间。她已把卡缪索连人带衣裳从床上推进书房，吩咐他立刻穿好，在里面候着。这场变戏法，全由一小方硬纸牌促成，上面印着几个字：德·莫弗里涅斯公爵夫人。王家内室引座官的女儿，一眼就看穿了全部局面。
“瞧！”女仆同谢内尔一起留在餐厅里，感叹道，“谁不说这是天上掉下个炸雷？主人正在书房穿衣裳；您可以上楼了。”
“这事一个字也不许说，记住。”谢内尔说。
此刻他自觉背后有一位贵夫人撑腰，而且救德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵的种种措施，已获得（口头的）御准，说起话来便带上了权威的口吻；这对卡缪索，比他原本会摆出的低声下气，不知管用多少。
“先生，”他说，“昨晚失口说出的那几句话，也许让您吃了一惊，可它们是当真的。

===Original===

The house of d’Esgrignon counts upon you for the proper conduct of investigations from which it must issue without a spot.”
“I shall pass over anything in your remarks, sir, which must be offensive to me personally, and obnoxious to justice; for your position with regard to the d’Esgrignons excuses you up to a certain point, but﻿—”
“Pardon me, sir, if I interrupt you,” said Chesnel. “I have just spoken aloud the things which your superiors are thinking and dare not avow; though what those things are any intelligent man can guess, and you are an intelligent man.﻿—Grant that the young man had acted imprudently, can you suppose that the sight of a d’Esgrignon dragged into an Assize Court can be gratifying to the King, the Court, or the Ministry? Is it to the interest of the kingdom, or of the country, that historic houses should fall? Is not the existence of a great aristocracy, consecrated by time, a guarantee of that Equality which is the catchword of the Opposition at this moment? Well and good; now not only has there not been the slightest imprudence, but we are innocent victims caught in a trap.”
“I am curious to know how,” said the examining magistrate.
“For the last two years, the Sieur du Croisier has regularly allowed M. le Comte d’Esgrignon to draw upon him for very large sums,” said Chesnel. “We are going to produce drafts for more than a hundred thousand crowns, which he continually met; the amounts being remitted by me﻿—bear that well in mind﻿—either before or after the bills fell due. M. le Comte d’Esgrignon is in a position to produce a receipt for the sum paid by him, before this bill, this alleged forgery, was drawn. Can you fail to see in that case that this charge is a piece of spite and party feeling? And a charge brought against the heir of a great house by one of the most dangerous enemies of the Throne and Altar, what is it but an odious slander? There has been no more forgery in this affair than there has been in my office. Summon Mme. du Croisier, who knows nothing as yet of the charge of forgery; she will declare to you that I brought the money and paid it over to her, so that in her husband’s absence she might remit the amount for which he has not asked her. Examine du Croisier on the point; he will tell you that he knows nothing of my payment to Mme. du Croisier.

“我倒好奇，怎么个落进圈套。”预审法官说。
“两年来，杜·克鲁瓦西耶先生一直照例允许德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵先生向他支取巨额款项，”谢内尔说，“我们拿得出10万多埃居的汇票，都是他一次次如数承兑的；款项由我汇付——这一点请您记牢——或在票据到期之前，或在到期之后。德·埃斯格里尼翁伯爵先生还拿得出一张收据，证明票款在这张所谓伪造的汇票开出之前已经付讫。这样一来，您还能看不出这份控告是出于挟私和党见吗？一个大家族的继承人，被王座与圣坛最危险的敌人之一加以控告，这不就是可憎的诽谤又是什么？这桩案子里有多少伪造，我的事务所里就有多少伪造。传唤杜·克鲁瓦西耶夫人吧，她对伪造的控告还一无所知；她会向您声明，是我把钱送来交到她手里，好让她趁丈夫不在，代他汇付那笔他还没有向她要过的款子。就这一点讯问杜·克鲁瓦西耶；他会告诉您，我付款给杜·克鲁瓦西耶夫人的事，他毫不知情。

She leant over her husband.
